This supersedes the 999 594 figure the earlier reading took from the first hit rather than the largest, and it is the number the 8 MiB attachment ceiling is set against |
| 248 | **A Claude image is a tool result 512 times out of 513, and nothing anywhere records a filename** | the same walk over 106 transcripts, collecting the key sets | **512 `tool_result.content[].image`** against **1 `message.content[].image`** — the paste. A paste is an `image` block beside the `text` block, `imagePasteIds` on the record, and its `source` carries exactly `{type, media_type, data}`: no name, no dimensions, the media type is all there is. A tool result carries an `image` in the result's content array **with no text sibling at all** — so a reader was shown a `Read` that finished and produced nothing — and its `toolUseResult.file` carries exactly `{base64, type, originalSize, dimensions}` on all 499 of them. **No filename in either shape**, which is why `att.name` is absent rather than invented. Largest record 1 364 056 bytes; largest transcript 27.8 MB |
| 249 | **`toolUseResult.file.originalSize` is not the number of bytes in the record** | compared it against the exact decode length of the `base64` beside it, on all 499 | Agrees on **199** and disagrees on **300** — 264 of them *smaller* than the record's own bytes and 36 larger (e.g. `originalSize` 140 171 against 267 841 decoded, at `originalWidth` 1080 → `displayWidth` 900). It is the size of the file Claude read, before it resized it for the model. So `att.bytes` is computed from the base64 in the record — `(len − padding) × 3 ÷ 4`, which is exact — and never read off `originalSize`, or a client would size a progress bar against a file it is not being sent |

## What cancellation and a cold pool actually do (scripted providers and a real store, no herdr)

The rows behind a three-model review of the whole tree. Every one of these was a defect that read
as a flake first: a test that failed once in a full suite and passed alone, twice over. They are
here because the fixes cite them, and because each mechanism was unlike its symptom — a stray frame
that looked like a leak was half an artefact, and a `database is locked` that looked like
contention was a pool option added four commits earlier. Measured with scripted providers and a
real `Store`, so none of it needs a herdr and none of it is load-sensitive.

| # | Claim | How | Result |
|---|---|---|---|
| 250 | **`JoinHandle::abort` does not stop a pump already inside an iteration: exactly one more frame reaches the outbox** | `pump_pane` against a scripted provider on a multi-thread runtime, fed a frame per `yield_now`, then `abort()` followed by `purge_pane` in the order `Session::unwatch` used them; 15 trials | A `grid.patch` for the stopped pane is pushed **after** the purge on the **first** trial, every time, and never more than **one**. The arm that takes an update off the watcher and calls `wire.send_update` has no await in it, so cancellation cannot land until the push is done — and emptying a queue cannot catch a frame that is not in it yet. This is why the stop is recorded in the outbox and checked under the same lock as the push, rather than being a purge |
| 251 | **A `grid.*` frame the node sent before it read an `unwatch` arrives after it, and is indistinguishable from a leak** | `resync_repaints_every_watched_pane_and_unwatch_stops_one` with its count split by a `ping`/`pong` barrier, ~25 runs of the live suite under three-way contention | One failure, and the stray was in the **pre-barrier** window: a blank 94×40 `grid.reset` for the watched pane, published by the resync's own repaint microseconds before the unwatch was dispatched. The post-barrier window was empty. Dispatch is sequential, so a pong sent after the unwatch is the client's only proof the pane has been dropped, and a count that starts before it is counting frames the node was still entitled to send |
| 252 | **A resync re-opened every pane it re-watched and published a blank grid over each** | `a_re_watched_pane_is_re_attached_rather_than_re_opened`; scripted provider, no herdr | `Session::watch` stopped the old pump before the new one watched, and the pump owns the registry's only `Watcher` — so the `PaneEntry` was evicted between the two and the re-watch took the slow path: fresh `Emulator`, fresh `observe`, and `reset_flush_after` publishing an **empty** grid at the pane's real geometry. Measured `opens=2` and one all-blank `grid.reset` on the **first attempt of three runs**: `abort` reliably beats the new pump's `registry.watch()`, so this fired on every pane of every resync rather than occasionally. The stitched `ScrollbackRing` was evicted with it, losing history above herdr's own read cap ([#51](#)). The blank stands only until the new observer's first frame, which is `full: true` ([#53](#), [#206](#)) |
| 253 | **Two sqlite connections doing the `journal_mode = WAL` transition on the same zero-length file both fail with SQLITE_BUSY, and `busy_timeout` does not cover it** | sqlx 0.9.0 / libsqlite3-sys 0.37.0; 400 rounds × 4 concurrent `SqliteConnectOptions{create_if_missing, journal_mode = Wal, busy_timeout = 5 s}.connect()` on a freshly created 0-byte file | **241 of 1600** returned `(code: 5) database is locked` — the rollback-to-WAL transition needs an exclusive lock and answers BUSY *without* invoking the busy handler, so the timeout is not consulted. `SqlitePoolOptions` reaches that state on its own with `min_connections(1)`: `connect_with` races the maintenance task sqlx spawns to fill the minimum, and on a runtime with an idle worker both open. **58 of 20 000** sequential opens on an idle 8-worker runtime, **0 of 64 000** at 64-way concurrency (saturated workers close the window, which is why it looked unreproducible), **0 of 20 000** with `min_connections` removed. Removing it costs nothing: with neither a lifetime nor an idle timeout set, nothing closes a connection anyway |
| 254 | **A hub re-opens a relayed pane on a resync once a client watches more than one, discarding its shadow and its stitched history and re-crossing the WAN** | scripted peer, 20 client `resync`s on the default multi-threaded runtime, counting requests that cross the link | **1 pane watched → 0 of 20 re-crossed; 3 panes → 20 of 20**, each pane costing an `unwatch` and a `watch`. On a `current_thread` runtime one pane re-crosses every time too. `PeerLink.panes` holds `Weak<RemotePane>` and only a watcher keeps one alive; `hand_over` aborts the old pump before the replacement watches, and `abort` is not synchronous, so tokio's LIFO slot normally polls the replacement first and a single pane survives on its own — but `Session::resync` spawns a pump per pane, and the second spawn displaces the first from that slot, so the aborted pump is reaped first and takes the pane with it. **0 of 20 with 3 panes** after `Peers::hold_while` and a `Hold::Peer` kept on the `PaneHandle`. The mesh twin of [#252](#), which by contrast fires unconditionally because the local stop is synchronous |
| 255 | **Google caches a `statements:list` verdict for its own `maxAge`, and the answer does not move until it expires** | `statements:list` against three hosts known to be configured correctly, reading back `maxAge`, plus one that is not | **`tiktok.com` 2974 s, `ebay.com` 2377 s, `shopify.com` 600 s** — and a *failed* lookup 600 s. It does not track the `Cache-Control` the site serves: shopify sends `max-age=86400` and got 600 s, tiktok sends `no-cache` and got 2974 s. The eight days in the revocation documentation is the maximum bound the API may impose, not what it returns. So `kampr doctor`'s asset-links check reflects a fix within about ten minutes and never sooner — its fixes say to wait rather than to re-run — and **"expose it briefly, let Google validate, then close it" is not a strategy**: the verdict expires inside the hour and the phone is refused again |
| 256 | **A hub does not serve an attachment for a pane it relays, and the refusal is the same 404 a stale id gets** | a real hub node with an enrolled peer advertising `01JATTACH/w1:p1` over a real mesh socket, asked `GET /api/attachment/{peer_node}/{local}/{id}` with a real device token — `kampr-node/tests/mesh.rs::a_relayed_pane_promises_no_attachment_this_hub_could_not_serve` | **404 `{"error":"no such attachment"}`**. The route resolves the pane through `Sessions::route`, which searches only this node's own herdr sessions, so a peer's pane has no transcript here and every id the peer minted is indistinguishable from a stale or hostile one — by design ([#233](#) is why that sameness is deliberate). Field symptom: a phone attached to the hub, watching a pane on a peer, was offered a screenshot button that answered *"The node no longer has this attachment"* on an image intact one hop away. The hub strips `att` from relayed `convo`/`convo.turn` now, so the button is absent rather than dead. Carrying the bytes instead would put a 2.22 MB record ([#247](#)) on the socket that carries frames, at the WAN hop, which is the head-of-line problem the attachment route exists to avoid |
| 257 | **Chunking an attachment onto a bulk lane keeps a pane repainting during the transfer; one message stops it for the whole transfer** | a real node session and outbox over a transport throttled to 1 MB/s, a 1 MiB attachment pulled by a scripted hub while `ping` ran every 20 ms — `kampr-node/tests/attachment_mesh.rs::a_pane_keeps_repainting_while_an_attachment_crosses_the_same_link` | Worst frame round trip during a **1.449 s** transfer, over 69 frames: **84.6 ms** as landed — 64 KiB chunks on a lane every frame overtakes, against the **83.3 ms** it takes to write one chunk, so a frame waits for the chunk already going out and for nothing queued. **354 ms** with the same chunks on the ordinary queue (the window of 4 sits in front of it). **1.318 s** — the whole transfer — sent as one message, which is the shape [#247](#) warned about and the reason `/api/attachment` is HTTP rather than a wire message. Both mechanisms are load-bearing: the window alone bounds memory, the lane alone bounds latency |
| 258 | **Serving a ceiling-sized file whole off `GET /api/attachment` costs about 5 ms and about 1.4× the body in peak RSS, so buffering is not what the ceiling is protecting** | an 8 MiB file on disk (the `MAX_BYTES` ceiling exactly) fetched through a real node's HTTP route over loopback with a real device token, `VmHWM` read either side — `kampr-node/tests/attachment.rs::a_file_at_the_ceiling_crosses_the_local_route_whole -- --nocapture`, 6 runs | **5.24–6.10 ms** end to end and peak RSS **+11.3 to +11.4 MiB** for an 8 MiB body — the read plus one copy into the response. So streaming this route would buy back about 11 MiB held for 5 ms on the machine the file is already on, and would cost a second code path whose `Content-Type` still has to hold the first bytes back to sniff them. What the ceiling is actually protecting is the **other** lane: a peer buffers the whole body before it chunks it ([#257](#)), so a file id crossing the mesh costs the peer the whole file at once, and raising the ceiling is a mesh measurement rather than an HTTP one |


## Which session a pane is on, and what the client is still holding (real `claude` 2.1.246 under a PTY; every `~/.claude/projects` on all three of the operator's machines)

The rows behind the operator's report: *"the conversation pane for claude doesn't match the open
claude in the terminal … how often does it double check its on the right session?"* Driven on
`comingclean` by forking a PTY, running a real `claude` in a throwaway directory and sending it
`/clear` and `/compact` with the session record and the project directory snapshotted either side
— **with every inherited `CLAUDE_*` variable stripped**, because `CLAUDE_CODE_CHILD_SESSION` turns
transcript saving off entirely and the first attempt measured a run that wrote no transcript at
all. Scanned on `pleader` and `giftofthemagi2` with the node's own `kampr-journal` compiled into a
throwaway binary, so the resolution measured is the one the node performs rather than a replica of
it.

| # | Claim | How | Result |
|---|---|---|---|
| 259 | **`/clear` opens a new transcript under a new session id and rewrites `~/.claude/sessions/<pid>.json` in place; `/compact` does neither** | one PTY-driven `claude` 2.1.246 sent a message, `/clear`, a message, `/compact`, a message, with `sessions/<pid>.json` and the project directory stat'd after each | `/clear`: **new file, new `sessionId`, same `pid`, same `procStart`**, and the session record is rewritten **0.1 s before** the new transcript exists. `/compact` leaves both alone — the same file grows 19 641 → 38 728 bytes across a `{"type":"system","subtype":"compact_boundary"}` record and a `user` record carrying `isCompactSummary: true`. So a pane's transcript moves on `/clear` and on a restart, and never on `/compact`. Two windows fall out of it: the record is written **at launch**, naming a session whose file does not exist until the first message — **7.2 s** here, and 4 m 20 s on a real pane on `pleader` — and after `/clear` there is a **0.1 s** window in which it names a file that is not there yet. In both, `locate_by_process` finds nothing and the search falls through to the working directory. The record is removed when the process exits, extending [#181](#) — which read the file for live processes but never watched one move |
| 260 | **A pasted image in the first message pushes a transcript's `cwd` past the head window, and the directory search then answers with the previous conversation in the same pane** | streamed every `~/.claude/projects/*/*.jsonl` on all three machines through `discover.rs`'s own bounds — first 256 KB, 40 lines, looking for `cwd`; last 256 KB looking for `timestamp` — then resolved the live pane on `pleader` with the node's own registry | **1 of 81 on `pleader`, 0 of 19 on `comingclean`, 0 of 154 on `giftofthemagi2`** are invisible to the head (plus 5 stub files of 267 bytes across the two, which hold one `bridge-session` record and nothing else). The one that matters is **13.5 MB and live**: the pane the operator was working in. Its first five records are headers that carry no `cwd`, and the sixth is the user's opening message with a base64 JPEG in it — **288 413 bytes, with `"cwd"` at byte 288 281**, so no complete record inside the 256 KB budget names a directory. Resolved on the machine: `locate_by_cwd` answered **`NotFound`** with the process-start bound and **the previous conversation in the same pane** without it, while `locate_by_process` answered correctly — so claude's session record was the only thing standing between the operator and the wrong transcript. A file inside `projects/<slug(cwd)>` whose head declares *no* directory is now taken as this directory's, since claude's own filing is the claim; one declaring a *different* directory is still refused, and the fallback sweep across every project and codex's flat `sessions/` still refuse silence. Guarded by `kampr-journal/tests/identity.rs::a_transcript_whose_first_message_is_too_big_to_see_past_is_still_this_directorys`, which fails in both directions |
| 262 | **Claude does emit parallel `tool_use` blocks in one record and their results come back out of order — twice in 145 815 records across three machines, and both in a sidechain the parser drops** | streamed every assistant record of every `~/.claude/projects` transcript on all three machines, including the `*/subagents/` directories, counting `tool_use` blocks per record; then read the two that carry more than one and matched their `tool_result` records | **1 of 57 074 on `pleader`, 1 of 71 315 on `giftofthemagi2`, 0 of 17 426 on `comingclean`** — two `Bash` calls in one record, both times. The results arrive in **separate `user` records and in the opposite order to the calls**, which is the whole point of running them in parallel and is exactly what breaks a settle that takes the first `Block::Tool` in the turn: the second call's state and line count land on the first call's card and the first call's own result overwrites them, leaving one card wrong and the other running for ever. **Both live instances are in `<session>/subagents/agent-*.jsonl`**, which `is_sidechain` drops and which no directory scan reaches anyway — so this has never been *reachable* on these machines. It is fixed as correctness rather than as a symptom: the harness emits the shape, and a non-sidechain one renders wrong. Every parser now records which block its call opened (`Turn::tool_block_mut(at)`); codex and agy build one turn per call, so their suites stay green with the defect restored and neither gets a test that would prove nothing | 
| 263 | **The fix that gave a second viewer of a peer's agent pane its transcript only fires when the hub's upstream watch is not already carrying one — so the case where it *is* was left, which is every joiner after the first and every `resync`** | a real hub, a scripted peer over a real mesh socket, one client watching a relayed pane with `conversation` and taking a page, then a second client watching the same pane — `kampr-node/tests/mesh.rs::a_viewer_joining_a_relayed_conversation_another_client_already_has_is_sent_one` | **Nothing crosses the link at all** — `next_but_ping` times out on "the hub asked for nothing". `PeerLink::watch` re-asked only on an *upgrade* (`conversation && !existing.conversation`), and a grid can be replayed from the hub's shadow while a transcript cannot: `convo` is passed straight through to whoever is subscribed at the time, and the peer sends a page when its pump **opens** a transcript and never again. So the joiner attaches to a stream whose page has already gone by, and agent panes open on the conversation (ADR 0005). Every joiner wanting the transcript now re-asks; the page that comes back is merged by id by the viewers that already had it. Mutation: restoring the `upgrade` guard leaves `a_second_viewer_asking_for_the_conversation_gets_an_upgrade_watch` **green** and this one red, which is the evidence the two are different defects rather than one. `resync` still costs no extra crossing for a pane watched without the conversation | 
| 264 | **A withdrawal cannot reach a client that comes back on a different socket, and no amount of server-side memory can — the node restarts too** | traced against the deployment: `pleader`'s node restarted twice inside one day (`journalctl --user -u kampr`), and `KamprConnection.session` re-watches every pane in `watched` on every reconnect while `KamprStore.paneStates` is a `getOrPut` map nothing prunes — then driven end to end in `kampr-node/tests/live.rs::a_page_a_reconnecting_client_could_not_have_been_told_about_replaces_what_it_holds` | The node's record of what a client holds lives with that client's session ([#261](#)), and a reconnecting phone gets a new one — so the turns are on the screen and the node cannot name them. Keying the record on the device instead would still lose it the moment the node restarts, which is a thing that happens. So the **page** says so: `convo` carries an additive `fresh`, set when the pump's first page is for a transcript this client is not already showing, and the client drops what it holds for the pane before applying it. `convo.load` answers are never `fresh` — they are older slices of the transcript already on screen, and marking them would throw away the page above. Four mutations, each red: the page never marked; every page marked; the client ignoring the field; the codec dropping it. For a **relayed** pane neither end can answer it — the peer's record is of what it sent the *hub* — so the hub marks the first page it hands each client for each pane, which is the one place that fact lives | 
| 261 | **The withdrawal that takes a stale conversation off the client lived with the pump, and the pump dies on every `unwatch` — so a `/clear` between leaving a pane's screen and returning to it was never withdrawn at all** | a real node, a real herdr, a real process in the pane and a real `~/.claude/sessions/<pid>.json`: watched a pane with `conversation`, took a page and a following revision, `unwatch`, moved the session as [#259](#) measures `/clear` moving it, then watched again — `kampr-node/tests/live.rs::a_conversation_that_moved_while_the_pane_was_unwatched_is_still_taken_off_the_client` | **The new page arrives with no withdrawal in front of it.** `applyConvo` in `PaneState.kt` merges by id and *prepends* what it does not recognise, and `KamprStore.paneStates` is a `getOrPut` map nothing ever prunes — so the new conversation lands above one that is still on the screen, and a conversation view sitting at its bottom shows the old session. The pump is created by `watch` and aborted by `unwatch`, which is what leaving a pane's screen does (`AppState.kt:372`), so its record of what the client holds went with it. The record now belongs to the client's pane entry and outlives the pump, and it is kept current at every send rather than only when a transcript is closed — an aborted pump never reaches its close. Three mutations, each red: sharing the record removed, the record taken at open removed, the record extended at each send removed. **Not fixed by this**: a client that *reconnects* re-watches on a socket the node has no memory of, and the node cannot know what that client is holding — the withdrawal there has to come from the client, or from a page that says it replaces |

## What `pane.zoom` actually does, and to whom (throwaway `kampr-probe-265`, real herdr 0.8.2)

The operator, verbatim: *"i dont even get what zoom at the desk is aha"*, and a previous reading
that its effect is invisible in Kampr. Both halves of that are wrong, and the reason is a
distinction nothing in the log had drawn: whether a desk client is attached. Driven with
`research/probe/rpc.py` against `herdr server --session kampr-probe-265`, and then again with a
real `herdr --session kampr-probe-265` forked under a PTY at 200x60 — **with every `HERDR_*`
variable stripped from the child**, because nested herdr is refused by default and the first three
attempts measured a client that had printed *"nested herdr is disabled by default"* and exited.

| # | Claim | How | Result |
|---|---|---|---|
| 265 | **`pane.zoom` really does resize the PTY, but only with a client attached — and the layout rect never moves either way, so the only pane geometry that follows a zoom is `scroll.viewport_rows`, and only when the split was vertical** | one workspace, one right split and one down split, `pane.zoom on`/`off` against each; `stty size` written to a file from inside each pane, `session.snapshot` rects and `pane.list` `scroll.viewport_rows` read either side; repeated headless and against a real 200x60 client | **Headless: nothing moves at all.** The PTY never followed the split in the first place (#68/#84) so there is nothing for a zoom to move: rect `47x40`, PTY `93x40`, unchanged through `on` and `off`, and `changed: true, zoom_changed: true` returned each time. **Attached, right split:** PTY `84x58` → **`171x58`** → `84x58`, while the rect stays `87x60` and `viewport_rows` stays `58` **in all three states**. **Attached, down split:** PTY `171x28` → **`171x57`** → `171x28`, rect stays `174x30`, and `viewport_rows` moves `28` → **`57`** → `28`. So the rect is fiction during a zoom exactly as it is in a headless session, and `viewport_rows` is honest exactly as [#84](#) found — it is the PTY's rows and not the rect's — but it says nothing about columns, and nothing on the socket does ([#221](#)) |

Which settles what a phone sees, without a new live test — and a new live test is why this is a
probe row rather than one. **`live.rs` runs headless**, so a zoom test there would drive an op that
cannot move a PTY, and would go green with the whole width path deleted: a harness that was never
the app ([#191](#)). What the chain is made of is already measured:

- **A down-split zoom is seen immediately.** `observe_geometry` takes rows from
  `scroll.viewport_rows`, the snapshot sweep notices `28 → 57`, `Stop::GeometryChanged` fires and
  the observer respawns at the new height. Guarded end to end by the width-following live tests.
- **A right-split zoom is seen by the width poll, and by nothing else.** Nothing in the snapshot
  moves, so `Stop::GeometryChanged` cannot fire — but herdr **re-wraps the pane the instant it is
  zoomed**, with no new output: the same 300-column logical line read back as rows of `84` before
  the zoom and rows of **`171`** after it, from a pane nobody had typed into. That is [#85](#)'s
  proof, so `probe_width` proves the new width on its next tick and `Stop::WidthChanged` restarts
  the stream. `width_poll` is **3 s**, which is the lag an operator is looking at, and it is the
  same poll-only story as a desk resize ([#52](#)).
- **A pane with no wrapped line has no proof**, and falls back to `max(rect, floor)` — the rect
  being the frozen pre-zoom one. It cannot crop content, because the floor is the widest row
  actually rendered and can only widen the stream, but the pane will not use its new width until
  something on it wraps.

**The naming, which is what the operator actually hit.** Kampr already has a control called Zoom —
the magnification of the rendered grid, in the pane header, announced as *"Zoom, currently 1.6x"* —
and the actions sheet offered a second chip called `zoom`, one screen away, for an unrelated herdr
op, with *"at the desk"* carrying the entire distinction. The sheet's chip is now **`fill the tab`**.


## What a browser says about the machine it is on (ChromeHeadless 151.0.0.0, Karma, `:shared:wasmJsBrowserTest`)

The operator, within minutes of 0.1.20: *"the wasm webapp is now showing the the keyboard buttons
(ctrl esc arrows etc.) - they stay for a while then go away weirdly."* Measured by dumping every
candidate reading out of the harness itself, which is the only browser this repository can drive.

| # | Claim | How | Result |
|---|---|---|---|
| 266 | **ChromeHeadless reports no input device at all, and reads as a *desk* under a `maxTouchPoints` predicate** | a throwaway `wasmJsBrowserTest` printing every pointer and hover reading Karma's browser gives | `maxTouchPoints: 0`, `ontouchstart: false`, `hover: none`, `pointer: none`, `any-hover: none`, `any-pointer: none`, `any-pointer: coarse: false`. So `(hover: hover) and (pointer: fine)` is **false** here, which is what the 0.1.20 comment recorded — but `maxTouchPoints > 0 \|\| (pointer: coarse)` is *also* false, so the reading that was chosen to keep the harness on the desk side does that by accident of a count, not by answering the question. It is the one browser in the matrix that answers **neither**, and folding that into either of the other two is what put a key row on an operator's desktop |

| 267 | **A real desktop reports ten touch points, so `maxTouchPoints` reads an operator's desk as a phone** | the operator's own desktop browser, the machine the 0.1.20 key-row regression was reported from — `[matchMedia('(hover: hover) and (pointer: fine)').matches, matchMedia('(pointer: coarse)').matches, navigator.maxTouchPoints]` pasted into its console | **`[true, false, 10]`.** The primary pointer is fine and hovers, nothing is coarse, and the hardware still counts **ten** touch points. So `maxTouchPoints > 0` — the 0.1.20 reading — is true on a machine driven by a mouse, which is the whole of the regression: the key row was drawn on a desk and then taken away again by the keydown latch on the first Escape. It also closes the desktop half of what [#266](#) left open, and confirms the media query answers correctly on real hardware where the harness could not ([#266](#) is `pointer: none` on all three). A touch count is a statement about what the hardware has; `pointer`/`hover` is a statement about how the person is driving it, and only the second is the question |

**What this row does not measure, and deliberately.** The values real desktop and real phone
browsers report for `hover` and `pointer` are defined by CSS Media Queries Level 4 — a desk's
primary pointer is `fine` and hovers, a touchscreen's is `coarse` and does not — and no browser on
this machine can be made into either. `BrowserKeyboardTest` therefore hands those readings to the
code as a table rather than asserting them off the harness, which is the honest shape: the headless
row above is measured, the desk and phone rows are the specification, and **the predicate is never
allowed to be chosen by what the harness happens to report.** That is the mistake this row exists
to record. Confirming a real desktop and a real phone is a device question and is listed as open.


## A pane that goes quiet while its connection answers (scripted provider and a real registry; ChromeHeadless 151.0.0.0, Karma, `:terminal:wasmJsBrowserTest`)

The operator, on the browser client against three nodes at 0.1.21: *"typing on the terminal via the
cli itself showed up on the wasm client (for the up to date claude session); typing on the terminal
via the wasm client showed nothing; the out of date codex session i couldn't see typing either way
but i could send via conversation panel and it worked - terminal still stayed dated"*, and,
earlier, ***"nothing was complaining and there was no reason why it would have conn issues"***.

Two independent defects, and the constraint that separated them is the third bullet: on the codex
pane the **conversation answered over the same socket** the grid had stopped arriving on. That is
per pane and per plane, not per connection.

| # | Claim | How | Result |
|---|---|---|---|
| 268 | **A watcher whose feeder has died parks for ever instead of reporting, and no surface can tell it from an idle pane** | `cargo test -p kampr-core --test registry a_watcher_whose_feeder_died_is_told_rather_than_left_parked` — a scripted provider, one watcher attached and caught up, then the provider's end of the `PaneEvent` stream dropped, exactly as `supervise` returning does | The watcher **timed out at 2s** with no error and no `Closed`. `PaneEntry` owns a `broadcast::Sender` of its own (`registry.rs`), so the channel outlives `pump`, and `WatchError::Closed` was **unreachable dead code** — constructed nowhere, asserted nowhere. `pump_pane`'s `let Ok(update) = update else { return }` was its only consumer and discarded it. The grid stops; the socket, the herd model, `watcher_count`, and the pane's own **conversation pump — a separate task over the same `Outbox`, and `Frame::plain` rather than `Frame::grid`** — all keep working. That is why one pane could be dated while its transcript answered. `hold_while` then pins the dead entry across a re-watch by design (#252), so closing and reopening the pane re-attached to the corpse and the stall survived the one recovery an operator has |
| 269 | **DOM focus is one slot for the whole page, and the browser pane claimed it exactly once** | `:terminal:wasmJsBrowserTest`, `BrowserInputTest.aComposerThatTookTheKeyboardHandsItBackWhenItIsDoneWithIt` and `.aButtonThatTookTheFocusDoesNotCostThePaneItsKeyboard` — focus the offscreen contenteditable, hand the focus to a real `textarea` and to a `button`, take each away again, and ask who holds it | The claim was asserted from a `LaunchedEffect` keyed on `keyboardOpen`, `focusRequests`, `surfaceSettled` and `enabled`. **None of those move when something else on the page takes the focus**, so the claim was never renewed: a `button` kept it indefinitely, and a `textarea` — the shape Compose's own text input takes in this DOM, and therefore what the conversation composer looks like from here — kept it and then handed it to the **body**, where nothing reclaimed it. The pane went on painting frames from the desk and took no keys, which is the report exactly, and the operator had **just used the conversation composer**. Only `reclaimKeyboard()` on a grid gesture recovered it, so an operator who never touched the grid never got it back. `focusout` alone does not fix it: the strand is the *second* move, and no event fires on an element that lost the focus some time ago |
| 270 | **Two codepoints an agent draws its tool-result tree with were absent from all four terminal faces, and the cut-in donor could not have supplied them either** | parsed the `cmap` of each shipped `terminalmono_*.ttf`; then took a codepoint histogram of three corpora — every pane of the live herdr read over `pane.read` while harnesses were working, 163 Claude Code transcripts (490M chars), 14 codex and 56 gemini sessions (118M chars) — and subtracted the face's coverage | All four faces carried **2197** codepoints and **none** carried `U+23BF ⎿` or `U+23AF ⎯`. Neither does **Noto Sans Symbols 2**, which the `Fonts.kt` comment names as the donor and which every one of the 834 existing cut-ins really did come from — so the stated build could never have closed this. The **live pane read is the only instrument that sees this class at all**: a transcript stores structured tool calls and the tree is drawn at render time, so `⎿` appears in `~/.claude/projects` almost only where an agent was discussing its absence. A harness **binary is not an instrument** and was rejected: 39 426 distinct non-ASCII codepoints, dominated by embedded Unicode property tables (NKo, Syriac, Armenian) that never reach a screen, and the noise does not filter out. A live screen carried 41 distinct non-ASCII with exactly three gaps — `⎿`, `⎯` and `U+29C9 ⧉`. Across all corpora, the class the face is built for (non-ASCII, one column, not a script letter) is **150** codepoints and **23** were missing. Guarded by `everyGlyphARealAgentScreenDrawsIsInAllFourTerminalFaces`, driven from `agent-glyphs.txt` rather than from a list of literals, red at *"terminalmono_regular is missing 23"* with the old faces restored |
| 271 | **The build was recovered by fitting the shipped glyphs, and the box lattice must be aliased rather than cut in** | drew every one of the 834 cut-ins in `terminalmono_regular` and its donor and solved for the transform; then rebuilt all four faces from JetBrains Mono NL 2.304 and compared | The rule is `scale = min(1, 560/ink_width, 900/ink_height)` about the baseline origin, then centred in the 600 advance — **440 of the 459 scaled glyphs land at ink width 560 and the other 19 at height 900**, and a rebuild reproduces **834/834 in all four faces** with no outline off by more than 1.5 units. The system JetBrains Mono NL 2.304 is outline-identical to the base that shipped across all 1363 shared codepoints, so the build is exactly reproducible and now is: `tools/terminalmono.py`. Cut-ins are the **same upright outline in all four faces** — Noto has no italic to draw from. **`⎿` and `⎯` are aliased onto `U+2514` and `U+2500` instead**: JetBrains' box glyphs deliberately overflow the cell (`└` spans x 250..620 of a 600 advance) so neighbours join, while a cut-in is centred in 560 and would land at x 103..496 — a tree that does not touch the rule beside it. Aliasing is also the only path that keeps the **weight of the face**. Faces went 2197 → **2220** codepoints, every added glyph at advance 600, vertical metrics untouched; `theTreeAnAgentDrawsItsResultsWithHoldsTheGridLikeTheBoxGlyphsItJoins` holds the pitch and proves the elbow is not a space |
| 272 | **~~Keypress-to-glyph is herdr's ~100 ms frame tick and~~ almost nothing else — the node's own share is below the noise floor of the measurement** (the tick clause is wrong and is superseded by [#273](#) and [#274](#); the readings and the node conclusion stand) | `a_keystroke_comes_back_as_a_glyph_and_the_round_trip_is_recorded`, 48 single characters through a real node and a throwaway herdr, against the same keystrokes driven straight at `pane.send_text` with `observe` on the other side and no node at all | Back to back on a quiet machine, release: **through the node p50 27.8 ms, p90 67.5, min 7.3, max 94.7**; **herdr alone p50 31.2 ms, p90 83.2, min 7.5, max 103.5**. The node path measures *faster*, so its contribution does not rise above the instrument — there is nothing to win in the node, and #22's p50 of 27 ms through `session control` was already this number. ~~The distribution is uniform in **[7, 100] ms**, which is a **~100 ms frame tick in herdr** that a keystroke waits half of~~ — **there is no such tick; the spread is the interactive `bash` these readings were taken through, and herdr's own share of them is ~1 ms ([#273](#), [#274](#))**. `observe` has no flag for a frame rate and `config.toml` no key, which is confirmed, and does not matter. **A fixed inter-keystroke delay aliases with whatever in the path is periodic and lies**: the first run of this used a flat 120 ms and reported a p50 of **98.7 ms**, three times the truth, tightly clustered because every reading landed in the same part of the phase. The test now jitters, and the row exists because the number did not. The only latency Kampr still owns is the browser's `withFrameNanos` input drain — up to one frame, ~16.7 ms at 60 Hz — which is **not** measured here |
| 273 | **The ~100 ms is neither herdr's renderer nor its `observe` API — it is the program in the pane, and on this machine that is `ble.sh`** | one throwaway herdr, one pane, one `observe` stream, and the same 40–100 rounds of `pane.send_text` → *the glyph comes back*, changing only what the pane runs. Then the same write timed against a **pull** (`pane.read visible`, polled) and against the **push** (the `observe` frame) at once; then against a real **attached desk client** driven on a pty beside the `observe` stream on the same pane at the same instant | Same herdr, same socket, same stream, three programs: **`cat` p50 1.2 ms** (min 0.7, max 1.3, n=45), **`bash --norc --noprofile -i` p50 1.2 ms** (n=16), **the operator's login `bash` p50 26.5 ms** (min 16.5, max 37.9, n=43) — which is #272's "herdr alone p50 31.2" reproduced exactly, and `~/.bashrc` line 5 sources `/usr/share/blesh/ble.sh`. The glyph is **not in herdr's terminal state either** until then: over 38 rounds the polled `pane.read` and the `observe` frame reported the same character within **±1 ms** (p50 −0.2, max 1.0), so the wait is upstream of both and nothing in herdr is holding it. And the API is not the slow client — on one pane at one instant, **attached desk client p50 2.96 ms** (min 2.53, max 3.26) against **`observe` p50 1.58 ms** (min 1.16, max 1.79), n=40 each: `observe` is *faster* than the screen at the desk, because it carries the pane's own diff while the client re-encodes the whole herdr UI. **There is nothing to take upstream and no knob to ask for.** The operator's own answer is to drop `ble.sh` or accept ~25 ms |
| 274 | **herdr emits a frame per PTY write, floored at 16 ms — that floor is its only tick, it is in the source as a constant, and it is not tunable** | a producer in a pane writing a self-timestamped line at a fixed gap, `observe` on the other side, inter-frame arrivals and *write-to-frame* measured against the producer's own clock; swept 250, 113, 50, 20, 5 and 1 ms. Then 45 s at a 2 ms gap for sequence continuity, and `herdr terminal session observe --help`, `herdr --default-config` and the disassembly of `herdr 0.8.2` for a knob | Frames track the producer exactly — **250 → 250.1, 113 → 113.2, 50 → 50.1, 20 → 20.1 ms** — and saturate at **17.1 ms** (min 16.0, max 18.2) for both the 5 ms and the 1 ms producer, ~58 Hz. Write-to-frame at the slow gaps is **p50 1.0 ms**. The number is in the binary: `herdr::app::runtime::<impl App>::next_loop_deadline_with_resize_poll` holds one timing constant, `mov $0xf42400` = **16 000 000 ns**, and `HeadlessServer::run::{{closure}}` compares against the same; the only 100 ms constant on that side is in `handle_scheduled_tasks`, which is housekeeping and not the frame path. **2610 records over 45 s, `seq` 1..2610 with no gap**, `full` on the first alone (#53 again), one record type `terminal.frame`, one key set. `observe` takes only `--cols` and `--rows`; `--default-config` has no frame-rate, refresh, tick or coalescing key anywhere in its 362 lines. `bytes` is accompanied by **`encoding: "ansi"`**, a field `observe.rs` does not read — kampr base64-decodes unconditionally, so a herdr that ever changed it would be rendered as garbage rather than refused |
| 275 | **An alt-screen pane keeps its rect and its `viewport_rows`, so `resolve_geometry` cannot park on one — the first of #268's four candidates is eliminated, and so are the third and fourth** | `session.snapshot` polled flat out — **~200 000 snapshots** — while one pane was driven through every state that could take its geometry away: `less`, `vim` and `htop` on the alt screen, a live `codex`, a background tab, a background workspace, a zoomed sibling, the 3 s after `workspace.create` and after `pane.split`, and a `server.stop` + respawn with the session restored. Then the `observe` stream of #274 read for record shape and sequence continuity across `clear`, alt screen in and out, a split and `server.reload-config` | **Not once.** An alt-screen pane stays in `layouts[]` with its full rect and `scroll.viewport_rows` **unchanged at the PTY's 40**; what the alt screen does move is `max_offset_from_bottom`, which reads 0 there and is the ring rather than the geometry — a `less` on the alt screen answers `pane.read recent lines: 5000` with the 40 rows of the viewport and nothing above it. `observe_geometry` returned `Some` in every one of those snapshots. A background tab costs one row of rect height to the tab bar (40 → 39) and nothing else; a background workspace and a zoomed sibling cost nothing; there is **no window at pane creation** (18 968 and 16 385 consecutive snapshots, zero) and **none across a herdr restart** (39 508, zero). The one state that does produce a missing rect is a pane whose process has **exited**, and there it is gone from `panes[]` too, so it is not this. Candidate three is eliminated for herdr 0.8.2: **2629 records, one type, one key set, zero unparsed**. Candidate four has no evidence behind it: **zero sequence gaps** in 2610 frames under a 2 ms producer and across every disturbance above. That leaves **only the width prober** — and `run_observer` can lose it only to a *panic*, since `run_observer` itself holds the sole `watch::Receiver` for as long as it runs; every path through `observe_cols`, `read_width`, `reading` and `rebuilds` was read for one and the only door left is a poisoned `widths` mutex, which would take **every** pane at once and the report was one pane |

**What this pair does not measure.** Neither #268 nor #269 reproduces the operator's session — no
herdr was driven, and what killed the codex pane's feeder in particular is **not established**.
#268 measures that *any* feeder death is silent and permanent, which is the property that made the
report possible whatever ended the stream; the candidates that can end it are all in `supervise`,
and #275 closes three of the four with a real herdr. The detection built on the back of this is
deliberately independent of which one it was.

| 276 | **Headless Chrome cannot be hidden from inside the page, so a hidden-tab defect has to be reproduced by removing the rendering opportunity rather than by hiding** | ChromeHeadless 151 under Karma (`:terminal:wasmJsBrowserTest`), probing `visibilityState`, `requestAnimationFrame`, microtasks, timers and `MessageChannel` from inside the page | Karma's own page reports `visibilityState: visible`; `window.open` leaves the opener visible and ticking at **32 rAF callbacks per 500 ms** (~15.6 ms a frame, which is the frame every keystroke was waiting for); a `display: none` iframe **still receives rAF** (`raf=1 micro=1 timer=1 channel=1 vis=visible`). So `BrowserInputTest` parks `requestAnimationFrame` and spoofs `document.hidden` instead, which is the stronger test anyway: it asserts delivery with *no rendering opportunity at all*, covering a throttled tab, an occluded window and a stalled compositor alike. Two further readings from the same harness: a Kotlin lambda passed as a `() -> Unit` parameter into a `js()` body **is** callable from JS under Kotlin/Wasm 2.4.10, and a wasm test returning `kotlin.js.Promise` **is** awaited by the Karma adapter — a deliberate late failure did fail the build |
| 277 | **herdr's own CPU: an `observe` stream costs it ~5 points of a core per pane and ~0 for a quiet one** — ~~and the cost tracks how hard the pane is *producing*, a flat-out one costing 73% of a core with nobody watching~~ **(the rate model and the 73% are superseded by [#280](#), which measures the variables as grid geometry and pane *visibility*, with rate nearly irrelevant; the observe-stream delta below reproduced and stands)** | `/proc/<pid>/stat` utime+stime deltas against a throwaway `herdr server --session kampr-cpu-probe-*` (deleted afterwards), stepped through: idle; a ~50 rows/s producer with no observer; the same with one `observe`; the same plus kampr's history poll at 100 ms and width probe at 3 s; and one `observe` on a quiet pane. Then a flat-out producer swept against 0, 1, 2, 3 and 4 simultaneous `observe` streams. Repeated read-only on the operator's live host (real `herdr server`, two agent panes, no client watching) with one `observe` opened on the probing agent's own pane | Throwaway: idle **0.10%**, producing-unobserved **0.40%**, producing + 1 observe **6.10%**, + kampr's polls **6.80%**, quiet + 1 observe **0.10%**. So the stream is **+5.7 points** and **every poll kampr makes adds +0.7 on top of it — 11% of the stream's cost**. Flat-out producer: **73.10%** at zero observers, then 80.25 / 82.25 / 89.50 / 94.75% at 1–4 streams — **~5 points each, linear, and the increment does not grow with output rate**, which is [#274](#)'s 16 ms floor doing exactly what it says. Four streams on a **quiet** pane: **0.12%**. Live host: baseline **3.45%**, with one observe **8.50%**, **+5.05 points** — the throwaway figure confirmed on a real workload. **An idle watched pane is free; the bill is one number times how many panes are producing while watched, and per [#274](#) there is no frame-rate knob to turn.** The log had no CPU measurement of herdr at all before this row |
| 278 | **Each of kampr's socket polls costs herdr well under a millisecond, so the whole polling budget is ~1.3% of a core and the process spawns round to nothing** | Same instrument as [#277](#): herdr CPU deltas divided by call count, driven at a known rate against a throwaway session — `session.snapshot`, `ping`, `pane.process_info`, a 4136-line `pane.read recent ansi` (the history poll), the width prober's two reads, and `herdr session list --json` timed as a spawn | **`session.snapshot` 0.167 ms, `ping` 0.033, `pane.process_info` 0.100, history read 0.300, width probe (2 reads) 0.400, `herdr session list` 0.52 ms wall / 0.39 ms child CPU.** For one node, four watched producing panes and one client that is **~43 calls/s → 13.0 ms/s → 1.3% of a core**, of which the 100 ms history poll is **12.0 ms/s** — nearly all of it by count, and still only +0.7 points of the +5.7 the stream costs ([#277](#)). Tripling the audience to three clients adds **~0.1% of a core**, because `SCROLLBACK_POLL` is the only per-client item: `registry.rs` refcounts one emulator, one `observe` and one history poller per **pane**, so the second and third viewer cost herdr nothing. Steady-state process spawns are **0.17/s → 0.007% of a core**. **Two premises that were in circulation are wrong: `settled` does not poll at a flat 20 ms (`manage.rs` doubles 20 → 320 ms and only runs per session op), and no row ever measured `herdr session list` at ~1 ms — [#240](#) measures spawn-to-listed *latency*, not cost** |
| 279 | **`/proc/locks` silently drops a record at its read boundary under concurrent lock churn, so one read of it is not evidence that a lock is absent** | The `agy` presence-lock tests, which take real `flock`s and ask `/proc/locks` which the process holds, run **2400 times at 6-way parallelism**; on a wrong answer, `holder` re-read the table immediately and printed both views | **16 wrong answers in 2400 runs (0.67%)**, and the re-read returned the missing lock every time: `raw (116 lines) [ino 6432783]` against `again (117 lines) [ino 6432773, ino 6432783]` microseconds later. The surviving record printed at index **89** in the lossy read and **90** in the honest one, which is the mechanism — `/proc/locks` is a `seq_file` whose iteration **restarts by index** each time the kernel's ~4 KiB buffer is drained, so a lock released before that boundary between the two `read` syscalls behind one `read_to_string` shifts every later record up one and the record *on* the boundary is never printed. The same restart can also print one record **twice**. **Consecutive reads are not independent** — the boundary sits at a fixed offset, so two reads that agree still failed **2 in ~5400**; agreement is the wrong operator and the union of four reads is what `presence.rs` uses. With it, **0 failures in 8800 runs**. This was a live defect, not a test artifact: a dropped record turns two held presence locks into one, so `holder` would occasionally name a conversation in exactly the case its contract says it must refuse — a node serving the wrong `agy` transcript for a pane |
| 280 | **herdr's CPU is paid per *visible* pane and scales with the desk's grid geometry, not with the pane's output rate — [#277](#)'s rate-based model does not transfer to a real desk** | `/proc/<pid>/stat` utime+stime deltas against throwaway `herdr --session kampr-cpu2-*` sessions spawned in a PTY at a chosen geometry (deleted afterwards), sweeping output rate at 292x72, frame rate at 292x72, the same 10 rows/s workload at 80x24, producing panes in background tabs, and 1/2/3 producing panes visible in one split. Server and attached client sampled separately | At **292x72**: idle **0.24%**; 10/50/200 rows/s and flat-out **5.64 / 6.36 / 7.36 / 6.60%**; **1 frame/s 6.45%**; full 72-row repaints at 5/15/30 Hz **6.25 / 6.25 / 6.50%**; a one-cell spinner at 10/30 Hz **6.45 / 6.35%** — **a ~6.3-point plateau that a 30x change in frame rate and a 20x change in row rate barely move**. The same 10 rows/s at **80x24** is **0.85%** (idle 0.10%). Two producing panes in **background tabs cost 0.72% against a 0.70% idle — a pane nobody can see is free**. Visible panes are linear: 1/2/3 producing in one split = server **6.36 / 12.64 / 19.08%**, client **2.24 / 4.60 / 7.12%**. **The variables are geometry and visibility; rate is nearly irrelevant.** [#277](#)'s flat-out **73%** did **not** reproduce — 6.60% here, ~11x lower. Caveat recorded honestly: that flat-out case may be instrument-limited (the harness drained the outer PTY at ~320 KB/s), but the rate-independence between 1 frame/s and 30 Hz repaints is not throughput-limited and stands |
| 281 | **An operator's "herdr is using 20% on a busy device" is herdr rendering two or three visible agent panes on a wide desk, and none of it is kampr** | Read-only on the live host: `ps --ppid` on the real `kampr serve`; `/proc/<pid>/stat` and `/proc/<pid>/task/*/stat` on the real `herdr server`; `pane.list` and `session.snapshot` over the socket; then the geometry/visibility sweep of [#280](#) reproduced on a throwaway at the host's exact 292x72 | Live host, one visible `claude` pane at 292x72 mid-task: herdr **1.50 / 1.48 / 1.51 / 1.57%** across four windows and **3.77%** in a later independent 30 s sample with the pane busier; kampr node **0.10–0.20%**; and **the real node has zero `observe` children — no stream is open at all**, so [#277](#)'s per-stream 5 points and [#278](#)'s 1.3% polling budget both contribute **nothing** to what the operator sees. Per-thread the work is the main thread (**0.70%**) and one `tokio-rt-worker` (**0.67%**); `herdr-pty-17`, the pane's actual PTY parse, is **0.03%** — **terminal parsing is not the cost**. Three visible producing panes at that geometry measure **19.08%** of a core in the server alone (26.20% with its client), which is the reported number. **The knob is how many panes are visible at once and how wide the desk is. Kampr is not in the bill** |
| 282 | **`accumulate_history`'s adaptive cadence is defeated in practice: a pane whose rate implies a 2 s poll is polled at the 100 ms floor, 20x too often** | A real `PaneRegistry` + `HerdrProvider` watching a throwaway session's pane driven at 10 lines/s, `RUST_LOG=kampr_core=debug`, reading the existing `debug!("history poll")` at `registry.rs:706` — `elapsed_ms` is the interval that actually elapsed, `next_ms` the one the policy chose | 413 polls in a 45 s watch. The poller **computed `next_ms=2000` on 397 of 413 polls (96%)** — `interval_for_rate(10.0)` = clamp(400/10 = 40.1 s, 100 ms, 2 s) = 2 s — and the interval that actually followed was **median 102 ms, mean 108 ms, max 203 ms**; it never once reached even 300 ms. **9.22 polls/s measured against 0.5 intended = 19.6x.** The mechanism is `Activity::saw_frame`'s `notify_one` on *every* frame: `Notify` stores a permit, so the `activity.woken.notified()` arm of the `select!` returns immediately and only the `fastest` floor is ever served. The comment says the wake is for output *starting*; it fires on every frame. **Cost is trivial — ~0.28% of a core per watched producing pane against ~0.015% intended, so fixing it wins ~0.26%** — but `HistoryStatus.poll` reports a cadence the poller never uses |
| 283 | **`SCROLLBACK_POLL` really does issue a second, redundant `pane.read` for a ring the history poller just read — and it is as small as it looks** | The [#282](#) harness with `session.rs`'s 3 s `registry.scrollback()` poll reproduced alongside the history poller, counting both and timing the redundant call; `registry.rs:277` → `herdr_provider.rs:843` inspected for any cache on the path | **14 `SCROLLBACK_POLL` calls against 364 history polls in 40 s = 3.8% more reads**, and the path has **no cache** — `PaneRegistry::scrollback` calls `provider.read_scrollback` unconditionally and reaches herdr's socket every time. At [#278](#)'s 0.3 ms of herdr CPU per read that is **0.011% of a core per watching client**: real, duplicated, and negligible. The bigger half is node-side, not herdr-side — **median 18.1 ms wall per call**, dominated by the ring ingest+render rather than the socket, or **~0.6% of a core in the node** per watching client |
| 284 | **A client whose peer freezes rather than closes is never reaped: the node serves its watches for ever, and every path the node has to notice is disarmed by a rule that is individually correct** | A confined node (`herdr.sessions = []`) against a throwaway session, watched through a proxy that keeps its sockets open and stops relaying — an app frozen in the background, a suspended laptop, a NAT that dropped the flow while both ends still believe it is up. `observe` children counted via `/proc/<node>/task/*/children`, socket state read with `ss -tni`, CPU by `/proc/<pid>/stat` deltas. First the eight cases where the node *does* learn the client is gone | **Every teardown path is sound**: `watch`→1 child, then `unwatch`, clean close, **abrupt TCP RST**, pane closed in herdr, and 40 rapid watch/unwatch cycles all return to **0** children within seconds and never peak above 1; four clients on one pane hold exactly **1**, released on the last `unwatch`. No zombies, no fd growth over ~50 cycles. **The frozen peer is the hole: the watch was still held after 1500 s and still there at 31 minutes.** `ss` says why — the producing socket sits in **zero-window persist** (`notsent:2636553`, `rwnd_limited:100%`, `backoff:15`), and every probe is ACKed with a zero window, which resets `icsk_probes_out` so `tcp_retries2` is never reached and it probes every 120 s **indefinitely**. The write never errors, so the writer never breaks, so `outbox.close()` never happens and `if outbox.is_closed()` never fires. The application valve cannot fire either: `Outbox::push` would close at `hard_cap`, but `pump_pane` **purges** grid frames on congestion rather than queueing them, so the queue never reaches it — **the backpressure rule that protects the node's memory removes the only application-level way it could notice a dead client**. There is no keepalive: the upgrade sets no idle timeout and `ping` is client-initiated only. **Cost measured: an abandoned observer costs exactly what a watched one does** (0 observers 2.62% of a core, 1 observer 13.78%, 1 *abandoned* observer 13.77%), plus **3.65–3.98%** of a core in the node spinning purge/re-encode for a reader that will never read, plus one of 64 socket permits held for ever. **The asymmetry is the finding: the mesh link between two servers on a LAN has exactly this guard — `PING_INTERVAL` 5 s, a `keepalive` that closes a peer which stops answering, and a test pinning it — and the link a phone uses has none** |
| 285 | **Every harness Kampr reads stamps a turn in UTC and says so — so `at` is an absolute instant, not a floating local time, and a clock face drawn from it is honest** | Read the newest transcript each adapter had written on this machine and printed the raw timestamp field: `claude` 2.x `~/.claude/projects/<slug>/<uuid>.jsonl` `timestamp`; `codex` `~/.codex/sessions/2026/08/26/rollout-*.jsonl` `timestamp`; `agy` `~/.gemini/antigravity-cli/brain/<id>/.system_generated/logs/transcript_full.jsonl` `created_at`. Cross-checked against `date -u` and against the node's own `updated_at` (`OffsetDateTime::now_utc().format(Rfc3339)`), and against the byte-for-byte `convo` frames in `client/conversation/src/commonTest/.../Fixtures.kt` | **All three write RFC 3339 with a `Z`**: claude `2026-08-24T13:15:30.868Z`, codex `2026-08-26T11:01:07.613Z`, agy `2026-08-26T05:19:26Z`. Codex is the decisive one — its *filename* is local (`rollout-2026-08-26T20-59-49`) while the field inside is UTC 11:01, so the two disagree by the offset and the field is the one that is zoned. Precision differs (ms, ms, whole seconds) and agy omits the fraction entirely; none of them omits the zone. **This retires the reasoning in `turnStamp` that a clock face "would be wrong by up to half a day"** — the offset is in the string. The client now reads it (`isoIsZoned`) and falls back to an age only for a stamp that names no zone, which no adapter in tree produces |
| 286 | **A `user` record is not the operator: 45% of the prose claude files under a user role is the harness talking to itself, and the conversation view rendered every word of it as "You:"** | Walked every transcript on this machine and classified each user-role text block by its leading top-level tag — claude `~/.claude/projects/*/*.jsonl` (22 files, `type=="user" && message.role=="user"`, `isMeta`/`isSidechain` already excluded by the adapter), codex `~/.codex/sessions/**/*.jsonl` (11 rollouts, `payload.type=="message" && role=="user"`), agy `~/.gemini/antigravity-cli/brain/*/.../transcript_full.jsonl` (3 brains, `source`/`type`) | **claude**: 259 prose against 214 `<task-notification>`, 12 `<command-name>` and 12 `<local-command-stdout>` — the notification is the single most common thing said in a user record after prose. **codex**: 140 prose, 16 `<environment_context>`, 2 `<recommended_plugins>`. **agy**: 0 — its only user source is `USER_EXPLICIT`/`USER_INPUT` and it is clean by construction. Every nested tag measured (`summary`, `task-id`, `event`, `usage`, `cwd`, `shell`, …) sits *inside* one of those five openers, so the top-level set is closed. Second-order and worse than the attribution: the client starts a new exchange at every user turn, so a background agent finishing mid-answer cut that answer into two replies. Adapters now strip the envelopes and drop a block that was nothing else; prose with a reminder stapled to it keeps the prose |
| 287 | **the operator's own hostname's public record now points somewhere Google can reach, and every party but one agrees — the phone is refused exactly as before** | Resolved the name from public resolvers (Cloudflare and Google DoH) and from the LAN (`getent hosts`); fetched `/.well-known/assetlinks.json` from the public address with `curl --resolve` so SNI and the chain are the ones Google sees; asked `digitalassetlinks.googleapis.com/v1/statements:list`; `apksigner verify --print-certs` on the 0.1.28 release APK; `kampr doctor` on the hub | **Split horizon**: public a public address, LAN a private address. The public fetch is `200`, `application/json`, **TLS verified**, and byte-identical to the LAN copy. `statements:list` returns the statement with no `errorCode` (`maxAge` 461 s). The APK has one signer, v2+v3, `a08a2184…c618`, matching both the served document and the hub's `[android]`. `doctor` is green end to end including `assetlinks` and `tier 1`. **[#170](#)'s instance is gone and Credential Manager still answers `RP ID cannot be validated`** — which is what sent this at [#288](#) rather than at the network |
| 288 | **Credential Manager's app→site half was never declared, so no published Kampr APK could have enrolled a passkey — and it is a build-time constant, so no generically distributed build can** | Read `AndroidManifest.xml` and every `res/values` directory in tree; `aapt2 dump xmltree --file AndroidManifest.xml` on the compiled APK; [Credential Manager's prerequisites](https://developer.android.com/identity/credential-manager/prerequisites) | The prerequisite is a `<meta-data android:name="asset_statements">` entry whose string resource lists the sites, one `include` per node — **checked in both directions**, and the app's direction is fixed when the APK is compiled. In tree there were **zero** occurrences, in source and in the compiled binary manifest, so the site half `doctor` proves is necessary and was **never sufficient**: every ceremony any installed build ever ran was going to end in `RP ID cannot be validated`. A hostname compiled into an APK is one an operator cannot hand to a stranger, which makes this structural rather than a configuration nobody found. The control is now hidden on a build declaring none, the manifest carries a commented-out template for a one-node build, and **a passkey in a browser needs neither direction** — a page's origin is its own RP ID. Supersedes [#171](#). Still unmeasured: no passkey has been created anywhere ([#116](#)) |


## What the CLI client has to be written against (herdr 0.8.2 on this desktop; throwaway `kampr-probe-w0-*` sessions, all torn down; ghostty 1.3.1, kitty 0.48.2, konsole 26.04.3 under kwin_wayland)

| # | Claim | How | Result |
|---|---|---|---|
| 289 | **herdr's whole default keymap, read out of a herdr that has never seen a custom config** — and one binding, copy mode, that `herdr --default-config` does not mention at all | `herdr --default-config` for the documented set, then a headless `herdr server --session kampr-probe-w0-defkeys` and a TUI client forked under a 150x120 PTY, both with `HERDR_CONFIG_PATH` pointing at a file holding nothing but `onboarding = false`, `ctrl+b ?` and PgDn through the whole keybinds popup, rendered off the client's own bytes with a minimal VT emulator. The `unset` rows are herdr's own word for a binding it ships unbound | **Prefix `ctrl+b`.** *global*: `prefix+?` keybinds - `prefix+s` settings - `prefix+q` detach - `prefix+shift+r` reload config - `prefix+o` open notification target. *workspaces and tabs*: `prefix+w` workspace navigation (`workspace_picker`) - `prefix+g` session navigator (`goto`) - `prefix+shift+n` new workspace - `prefix+shift+g` new worktree - `prefix+shift+w` rename workspace - `prefix+shift+d` close workspace - `prefix+c` new tab - `prefix+shift+t` rename tab - `prefix+p` previous tab - `prefix+n` next tab - `prefix+1..9` switch tab - `prefix+shift+x` close tab. *panes*: `prefix+v` split vertical - `prefix+-` split horizontal (`split_horizontal = "prefix+minus"`) - `prefix+x` close pane - `prefix+shift+p` rename pane - `prefix+e` edit scrollback - **`prefix+[` copy mode** - `prefix+z` zoom pane (legacy alias `fullscreen`) - `prefix+r` resize mode - `prefix+b` toggle sidebar - `prefix+h`/`j`/`k`/`l` focus pane left/down/up/right - `prefix+tab` cycle pane next - `prefix+shift+tab` cycle pane previous. *shipped unbound*: `open_worktree`, `remove_worktree`, `previous_workspace`, `next_workspace`, `switch_workspace`, `previous_agent`, `next_agent`, `focus_agent`, `move_tab_previous`, `move_tab_next`, `last_pane`, `resize_pane_{left,down,up,right}`. *navigate mode*, which is a **second keymap** that wins only while `prefix+w`/`prefix+g` is open and takes no prefix: `esc` back - `up`/`down` workspace list - `h`/`j`/`k`/`l`/`left`/`right` move focus - `tab`/`shift+tab` cycle pane - `enter` open workspace - `1..9` switch workspace. *`herdr --remote` only*: `ctrl+v` `remote_image_paste`. **`copy_mode` is real and rebindable but undocumented** — it appears nowhere in `herdr --default-config`, yet `herdr config check` accepts `copy_mode = "prefix+u"` (it rejects `keys.bogus_action` by name, so acceptance is not laxity) and the popup then reads `prefix+u copy mode`. **The operator's own `config.toml` differs from these defaults in exactly two places** — `last_pane = "prefix+space"` and `switch_workspace = "prefix+shift+1..9"`, both shipped unbound — which is the whole reason this row was measured against an isolated config rather than this machine's |
| 290 | **`ctrl+b ctrl+b` really does send one literal `ctrl+b`, and herdr's three modal footers are the rest of its grammar: copy mode and resize mode each swallow the keyboard with their own binds and no prefix** | `cat -v` in a throwaway `kampr-probe-w0-keys` pane, a TUI client forked under a PTY, `\x02\x02` written to the client's PTY and the pane read back with `pane.read source=visible`; then `\x02`, `\x02[` and `\x02r` with the client's status line rendered after each | `cat -v` printed **`^B`**, once — so the second `ctrl+b` is delivered as a byte and nothing else is. herdr says so itself in the **PREFIX** footer: `esc cancel - ctrl+b send prefix - w workspace nav - ? keybinds`. **COPY** mode's footer is `h/j/k/l w/b/e { } move - / ? search - n/N repeat - v/space select - y/enter copy - q/esc exit` and **RESIZE** mode's is `h/l width - j/k height - esc done`. Both are modal: while one is open every key in it is herdr's, the prefix is not involved, and `esc` is the only way out that both share. A client that routes on "prefix, then one key, then back to the pane" is wrong for these two — [#289](#)'s `prefix+[` and `prefix+r` open a state that persists until it is closed |
| 291 | **XTWINOPS is not a fit strategy on this desktop: the operator's own terminal ignores `CSI 8;rows;cols t` outright, and the one emulator here that honours it does not clamp — it will hand a client a grid four times wider than the display** | A python probe run inside each emulator from its own `-e`, holding `/dev/tty` in raw mode: read `TIOCGWINSZ`, install a `SIGWINCH` handler, query `CSI >0q`/`CSI 18t`/`CSI 14t`/`CSI 16t`, then write `CSI 8;r;c t` and poll `TIOCGWINSZ` every 5 ms for 3 s, re-querying `18t` and `14t` after each. Grow (30x100), shrink (20x60), absurd (400x900) and restore, in that order. `ghostty +new-window -e ...` is the only launch that works here — plain `ghostty -e` hangs against the running single-instance daemon and never opens a surface. Compositor is `kwin_wayland` (KDE Plasma, floating, 2560x1440), so nothing was tiling the windows | **ghostty 1.3.1-arch2.2 — ignores it.** Five requests, `first_change_ms: null` on every one, zero SIGWINCH, `18t` unchanged at 34x98 throughout. **kitty 0.48.2 — ignores it.** Same: no change, no SIGWINCH, `18t` pinned at 23x80. **konsole 26.04.3 — honours it completely**, grow *and* shrink, landing in **under 5 ms** (the poll's own granularity) with exactly **one SIGWINCH** per request and `18t` agreeing every time. But asked for 400x900 it **gave 400x900** and answered `CSI 14t` with `6000;7200` **pixels** — on a 2560x1440 display, with a measured 8x15 px cell. There is no clamp, so a client that asks for a 171-column pane on a narrow display gets it and the operator sees a slice. Second trap in konsole: `TIOCGWINSZ`'s `xpixel`/`ypixel` stay **stale** at the pre-resize values while `CSI 14t` is honest, so a client that reads pixels from the ioctl is reading a lie. **All three answer the queries**, which is the part a client can rely on: `CSI 18t` -> `CSI 8;rows;cols t`, `CSI 14t` -> `CSI 4;h;w t` in pixels, `CSI 16t` -> `CSI 6;h;w t` for the cell; **`CSI 15t` (screen size) is answered by none of them**. `CSI >0q` names the emulator and version in all three (`ghostty 1.3.1-arch2.2`, `kitty(0.48.2)`, `Konsole 26.04.3`), which is how a client can know what it is talking to before it tries. **wezterm, foot, xterm and alacritty are not installed on this machine and nothing here was measured about them** |
| 292 | **herdr's observe frames carry no mouse mode at all, and neither does any other surface on its socket — a node cannot tell a client whether a click means anything, and there is no near miss to build on** | `herdr terminal session observe w1:p1 --cols 100 --rows 30` against a throwaway `kampr-probe-w0-mouse`, every frame's base64 decoded and scanned for `ESC[?<n>h/l`, while the pane was driven through `printf '\033[?1000h\033[?1002h\033[?1003h\033[?1006h'`, the matching resets, `vim -u NONE -c 'set mouse=a' -c 'set ttymouse=sgr'`, and back to the shell. Then `pane.get`, `pane.list`, `pane.layout`, `pane.process_info` and `session.snapshot` read with vim live, and `grep -i mouse` over `research/herdr-api-schema.json` | **The whole private-mode vocabulary of the stream is four sequences: `?2026h`, `?2026l`, `?25l`, `?25h`** — synchronised update and cursor visibility, herdr's own, in 24 of 24 frames. **1000, 1002, 1003 and 1006 never appear**, set or reset, however the pane's program asks for them. That is not a gap in the encoder, it is [#23](#)/[#25](#): observe emits grid state, not a byte replay, so a mode change is consumed by herdr's emulator and has nothing to be re-emitted as. Nor is it anywhere else: `pane.get`/`pane.list`/`session.snapshot` carry no such field, and the **one** occurrence of `mouse` in the entire 91-method schema is `pane.graphics.info`'s `pixel_mouse`, which is a **decoy** — it needs `experimental.kitty_graphics` and an attached client with a known cell size to answer at all, and it then reads `true` **constantly**, unchanged across `?1000h`, `?1002h`, `?1003h`, `?1006h`, `?1016h`, `?1049h` and their resets and across a live `vim` with `mouse=a`. It describes the *host* client's pixel-mouse capability, not the pane's program. So [#9](#) is the only half that exists: a client can *send* an SGR mouse report as raw bytes through `pane.send_text`, and nothing in herdr will tell it when that is safe |
| 293 | **Nor does the alt screen appear: 1049 and 47 are as invisible as the mouse modes, and `max_offset_from_bottom == 0` remains the only signal there is — ambiguous exactly as it looked** | The [#292](#) observe capture, with the pane taken in and out of the alt screen four ways — `vim -u NONE`, raw `printf '\033[?1049h'`, raw `printf '\033[?47h'`, and their exits — after 300 lines of scrollback had been built, sampling `pane.get`, `pane.layout`, `pane.process_info` and `pane.read` at every step | **`?1049` and `?47` never appear in a frame** — the stream's private modes stay [#292](#)'s four. What *does* move is the ring: `max_offset_from_bottom` **262 -> 0 -> 263 -> 0 -> 264 -> 0 -> 265** across entry and exit, so `?47`/`?1047` swap the buffer in herdr exactly as `?1049` does and both restore the ring intact on exit ([#30](#), for all three). Everything else holds still: the layout rect stays 94x40, `viewport_rows` stays 40, and `pane.read source=recent lines=500` degrades to exactly `viewport_rows` lines. **No disambiguator exists.** `foreground_processes` is not one — a raw `?1049h` typed at a shell leaves it reading `bash` with the ring at 0 — and `pane.read`'s fourth source, `detection`, is just `visible` under another name. A pane that has never scrolled and a pane on the alt screen are the same pane to every question herdr answers, which is the ambiguity `scrollback_rows == 0` already carries and there is nothing behind it to resolve it with |
| 294 | **`pane.report_metadata` is not another [#75](#): it lands, it survives, and a name Kampr computes really does appear at somebody else's desk** | A throwaway `kampr-probe-w0-desk3` under an isolated config with `show_agent_labels_on_pane_borders = true`, two panes from `pane.split direction=right`, each given `pane.report_agent` and then `pane.report_metadata {source:"kampr", title:"zulu (cargo test)"}`; a TUI client forked under a 120x30 PTY and its bytes rendered. Then a second session with `[ui.sidebar.agents] rows = [["state_icon","workspace","$proj"],["$cost","agent"]]` and `tokens {proj, cost}` reported to three agent panes | **The reported title is the pane's border at the desk**: `+- zulu (cargo test) ------+` and `+- alpha (cargo test) -----+`, drawn side by side. Over the API it arrives as a new `title` key on `pane.get`, `pane.list` and `agent.list`, and it is **durable** — it survived pane output, an `OSC 0` title change from the program (which lands separately on `terminal_title`), a `pane.rename` (which lands separately on `label`) and a re-focus. `display_agent` replaces the agent's name in the agents sidebar (`zulu-agent` in place of `claude`). **Reported `tokens` render too**, as `$name` rows: the sidebar drew `deskws - zulu` over `9.50 - claude`. Two limits. `title` is **not** a sidebar row builtin — the builtins are `state_icon`, `state_text`, `workspace`, `tab`, `pane`, `agent`, `terminal_title`, `terminal_title_stripped`, and putting `"title"` in `rows` makes herdr reject the **entire config** with *"config.toml invalid; using defaults"*, not just that row. And `state_labels` is a closed vocabulary — `idle`, `working`, `blocked`, `unknown`, `done` are accepted and anything else is `invalid_state_label: unknown state label: phase`; each call **replaces** the whole map rather than merging it. So the desk half of W9 is real, but what an operator sees past the pane border is their `rows` config, not Kampr's to choose |
| 295 | **What arbitrates two reporters is not what the schema's field names suggest: it is last-writer-wins over a per-source table, `seq` guards only its own source, `ttl_ms` falls back rather than clears, and `applies_to_source` is a silent conditional that returns `ok` when it does not fire** | One pane in a throwaway `kampr-probe-w0-meta`, driven through `pane.report_metadata` from four made-up sources with `pane.get` read after every call: two sources alternating, then `seq` 10/5/11 within one source, then `ttl_ms: 1500` sampled 2.2 s later, then `clear_title`, then `applies_to_source` with and without a `pane.report_agent` authority in place | **There is no source priority — the most recent write wins**, whichever source made it. **Each source keeps its own record**, and this is the part a schema reading misses: `clear_title` from the source that was showing did not blank the pane, it **revealed the other source's title**. `ttl_ms` behaves the same way — a 1500 ms report was showing at +0.3 s and at +2.2 s the pane had fallen back to the previous source's value, so a TTL is an expiry of one record, never a clear of the field. **`seq` is a per-source monotonic guard, and a stale report is dropped silently**: `seq=5` after `seq=10` returned `{"type":"ok"}` and changed nothing; `seq=11` applied. **`applies_to_source` is a guard on the pane's agent authority, not a way to write into another source's record.** With authority held by source `A`, a report from `C` carrying `applies_to_source: "A"` applied and one carrying `applies_to_source: "Z"` did not — and both were acked `ok`. Worse, the one that did not fire left `C`'s record empty, so the pane fell back to a *third* source's title: a guard that misses does not leave the previous value alone, it withdraws the reporter. **`ok` on this method means "well-formed", not "applied"** — the only honest confirmation is to read `pane.get` back. (`agent` alone is not settable: `invalid_metadata_request: missing metadata field to set or clear`.) |
| 296 | **A token Kampr reports really does become the field herdr's own agents sidebar sorts and filters on — and `agent.list` shows none of it, so the API cannot see the view it just set** | Three panes in a throwaway `kampr-probe-w0-meta`, each `pane.report_agent`ed and then given `tokens {proj, cost}` = `{zulu, 9.50}`, `{alpha, 0.10}`, `{mike, 4.20}`; `agent.view.set {source, sort:[{field:{token:"cost"}, order}], label}` and a token-valued `filter`, with the sidebar read out of a TUI client forked under a 120x45 PTY and rendered, and `agent.list` polled alongside | **It works, exactly as the schema implies.** `cost` descending ordered the sidebar `a0`(9.50), `a2`(4.20), `a1`(0.10) with the token-less pane last; `cost` ascending inverted it; `{token:"proj"}` ascending gave `alpha`, `mike`, `zulu`; and `{op:"eq", field:{token:"proj"}, value:"alpha"}` left **one** row on screen. The `label` replaces the sort-mode word in the section header — `grouped` became `cost desc`, then `only alpha` — and `agent.view.clear` restored the default order. **The catch is that this is the TUI's view and nothing else's**: through all of it `agent.list` returned the same four agents in the same order, unfiltered. A client cannot read back the view it set, cannot use `agent.view.set` to shape its *own* list, and must sort locally regardless — which is what [`11-cli-briefs.md`](./11-cli-briefs.md) W2 already says to do. What this buys is the other direction: Kampr can make somebody's desk sort by a Kampr-computed field |
| 297 | **`pane.process_info` names the job on a plain shell pane, argv and all, for every process in the pipeline — and on the operator's own machine it will name nothing, because ble.sh keeps the command in the shell's process group** | One pane, `exec`'d into three shells in turn, `pane.process_info` read 2 s into a long-running foreground command each time: `/bin/sh`, then `bash --norc --noprofile`, then that same bash after `source ~/.bashrc` | Under `/bin/sh`: `sleep 10` -> `{name:"sleep", cmdline:"sleep 10"}`; `python3 -c ... --flag a1` -> the full `cmdline` with every argument; `sleep 9 \| cat` -> **both** members, `[{sleep, "sleep 9"}, {cat, "cat"}]`. Idle, it names the shell. Under `bash --norc --noprofile`, identical. Under the **same** bash after sourcing the operator's `.bashrc` — line 5 of which is `source /usr/share/blesh/ble.sh` — `sleep 10` reports `{name:"bash", cmdline:"bash --norc --noprofile"}` and `foreground_process_group_id` never leaves `shell_pid`. Nothing about herdr changed between the two readings; **ble.sh is the mechanism**, and it is on every interactive shell on this machine. `vim` is still named correctly under it, so the failure is specific to jobs ble.sh runs in its own group rather than to the reader. W9's `{cmd}` token is therefore real but **not universal**, and the response is richer than the schema says — `foreground_processes[]` carries `{pid, name, argv, cmdline, cwd}`, with `cmdline` pre-joined |
