| 298 | **`control` does not refuse a pane whose session has a desk client attached, and it does not evict it either — it silently reshapes the PTY underneath a TUI that goes on drawing the old box, which is worse than both** | A throwaway `kampr-probe-w0-desk3` with a real TUI client forked under a 130x36 PTY and a two-pane split rendered, then `herdr terminal session control w1:p1 --cols 70 --rows 20` from a second process while that client stayed attached; `pane.layout` and `pane.get` sampled before, during and after, a 60- and a 69-column line written into the pane during control, and the client's own bytes rendered | **No refusal.** `control` attached and streamed frames while the desk client held the session. The pane's layout rect stayed **52x35** — herdr's TUI never learned anything had happened — while `viewport_rows` went **33 -> 20** and the PTY's width became the controller's 70. The desk then drew a 50-column content box around a 70-column PTY: the 69-column line came back from `pane.read source=visible` at its full length and appeared on the desk **cropped at 49 with the tail gone**. `terminal.release` restored `viewport_rows` to 33 within a second, as [#19](#) says. So [#21](#)'s *"already has an attached client"* is about a second **controller of the same terminal stream**, not about a desk TUI at all, and every one of [#17](#)-[#21](#) having run headless is exactly why that read persisted. This is the ADR 0002 invariant with a face on it — the side effect of one person viewing a pane is another person's screen going wrong, silently, with no error anywhere and nothing in herdr's UI to say why |

## What the terminal itself will answer (kitty 0.48.2, ghostty 1.3.1-arch2.2, konsole 26.04.3, real windows on the operator's Wayland session)

| # | Claim | How | Result |
|---|---|---|---|
| 299 | **konsole answers kitty's graphics query and renders a kitty graphics escape, so the in-band probe beats a name table — and the name table written from [#291](#) was already stale on the machine it was written from** | Driven inside each emulator from its own `-e`, `/dev/tty` in raw mode: write `ESC _ Gi=31,s=1,v=1,a=q,t=d,f=24;AAAA ESC \` then `CSI 16t` then `CSI c` as the fence, read the answers, then write a real `a=T,f=100,c=20,r=5,i=77,q=0` PNG payload and read the placement ack. Windows opened for the probe and closed after, as [#291](#) was | **All three answer `_Gi=31;OK`** — kitty, ghostty **and konsole** — and all three ack the placement `_Gi=77;OK`, so all three render kitty graphics. **DA1 advertises sixel (attribute 4) on konsole alone**: `?62;1;4`, against kitty's `?62;52;` and ghostty's `?62;22;52` — and it is a *parameter*, not a substring, so a client matching `4` anywhere in `?64;22c` would claim sixel on a terminal that has none. `CSI 16t` cell sizes differ across all three: konsole 8x15 (agreeing with [#291](#)), ghostty 8x17, kitty 8x18, so a client that assumes a square cell or a shared one scales every image wrong. A name table would have handed konsole sixel; the probe hands it kitty graphics, which it demonstrably prefers. Separately, `sixel2png` (libsixel — an independent decoder, not the one the encoder uses) decoded the sixel payload back to 200x100 with every quadrant placed and within 2/255 on every colour, so the sixel path is proved by a decoder that shares no code with it |
| 300 | **crossterm's `EnableMouseCapture` turns on 1003 any-motion and 1015 urxvt as well as 1006, so a client's own event stream carries motion with no button held — forwarding it verbatim would flood a pane with reports its program never asked for** | Read `crossterm-0.29.0/src/event.rs:322-334`, the `write_ansi` for `EnableMouseCapture`, against its disable at 355-362 | It writes **`?1000h ?1002h ?1003h ?1015h ?1006h`** and resets the same five. 1003 means `MouseEventKind::Moved` arrives on every cell crossing with no button down; 1015 means a host that ignores 1006 answers in urxvt form instead. So the modes a client *receives* under are not the modes it should *send* under: `kampr-tui`'s `mouse.rs` ignores `Moved` and derives motion only from `Drag`, which is 1002 semantics whatever the host was asked for, and encodes outbound reports as SGR 1006 regardless — the shape every mouse-aware program of the last decade accepts. Nothing here is herdr's behaviour; it is the client library's, and it is recorded because a client that forwarded its own input modes would be sending [#292](#)'s missing information back into a pane that never asked |

## What herdr will let a client do to somebody else's sidebar (throwaway `kampr-probe-w9-view`, real herdr 0.8.2)

| # | Claim | How | Result |
|---|---|---|---|
| 301 | **`agent.view.set` answers with the view rather than `ok`, and its sortable vocabulary is two builtins and a reported token — so there is no way to sort a desk by a *title*, and the naming feature's two halves are structurally dependent rather than merely related** | `research/probe/rpc.py agent.view.set` against a throwaway `kampr-probe-w9-view`, 40 parameter shapes, torn down after | The reply is `{"type":"agent_view","active":true,"source":…,"label":…}` — it echoes source and label and **never the sort**, so a caller learns a view is active and not what it is. `source` is required, non-empty, ≤120 chars, `[A-Za-z0-9:._-]`; `label` non-empty and **≤32 characters** — both `invalid_agent_view`. `order` is `asc`/`desc` and nothing else (`unknown variant 'ascending'`). `field` is an untagged enum taking `{token:"…"}` or a bare builtin, and of **29** plausible builtins tried **exactly two are accepted: `agent` and `status`** — `{builtin:"agent"}`, `{metadata:"title"}`, `"cost"`, `"title"`, `"workspace"`, `"cwd"` all answer `did not match any variant of untagged enum AgentViewSortField`. So a desk can only be sorted by a name Kampr computed **through a reported token** ([#294](#)), which is why `sort_desk_agents` refuses rather than implies `report_to_herdr`. An unknown extra key *inside* a sort entry is silently accepted and ignored. There is no `agent.view.get`: the method list carries `set` and `clear` and no reader, confirming [#296](#) at the API surface |
| 302 | **`agent.view.clear` carries no source and clears whatever is active, whoever set it — so a node that clears unconditionally on the way out throws away a view the operator set for themselves** | Same session; `agent.view.clear` with `{}`, with `{"source":"kampr"}`, and with `{"source":"nobody"}` | All three answer `{"type":"agent_view","active":false}`; the parameter is accepted and **ignored**. With [#301](#)'s write-only reply this is a last-writer-wins global with no reader: a node cannot ask whether the view it is about to clear is its own. A client must therefore track what it sent and clear **only** that — which is what `DeskAgents::sweep` early-returning on "sent nothing" is for |
| 303 | **Reported `tokens` behave unlike a reported `title`: they merge across sources into one map and they read back, which makes them the one half of `pane.report_metadata` with honest confirmation available** | `pane.report_metadata` from sources `kampr`, `k2` and `k3` on one pane, `pane.get` after each | `tokens` is `{String:String}` — `{"cost":9.5}` is refused with `expected a string`. `pane.get` returns the **merged** map: after `kampr` reported `{kampr:"kampr (cargo test)"}` and `k2` reported `{a:"b"}`, the pane carried both. A same-named token from a second source overwrites that key alone. So unlike `title`, which is last-writer-wins over the whole field ([#295](#)), a token one source reports is not withdrawn by another's report — and it is readable back, where `title`'s only confirmation is re-reading `pane.get` and the view's is nothing at all |

## What a demotion does not reach in time (scripted node and a second store on one file)

| # | Claim | How | Result |
|---|---|---|---|
| 304 | **A hub demoted out of process was served an arbitrary file read for a full recheck interval, because the one verb that reads any path on the host was the one not gated like typing** | `att.fetch` with a `FileRef` id on a `Caller::Hub` session, the device row set to `readonly` by a **second SQLite connection** immediately beforehand — a real out-of-process gesture, so no in-process broadcast can help it (`crates/kampr-node/tests/attachment_mesh.rs`) | The hub was handed `att.open`, **every** `att.chunk` and `att.end` — the whole file — and the `role` frame announcing the demotion arrived *after* them, on the next 2 s `DEVICE_RECHECK` tick. The dispatch-time refresh covered `manage`, `input` and `answer`; `att.fetch` was absent from it. That is the inverse of the argument the file-id form rests on: it is allowed **because** it is equivalent to typing into the terminal, and it was the one path not re-reading the device row the way typing does. Now in the same arm, and the test demotes through a second store rather than through the node's own API so it measures the path an operator's `kampr setup` actually takes |

## Claiming a PTY on purpose, and what it costs (throwaway `kampr-probe-size`, real herdr 0.8.2)

Measured for [ADR 0012](./adr/0012-one-deliberate-resize-behind-a-panel.md), which narrows
[ADR 0002](./adr/0002-kampr-never-resizes-a-pane.md). [#219](#) established that a controller's size
outlives a **killed** controller on a headless pane; these two ask the questions that decision
actually rests on.

| # | Claim | How | Result |
|---|---|---|---|
| 305 | **A controller's size survives its own *clean exit*, and enlarging works exactly as shrinking does** | a headless `herdr server --session kampr-probe-size`, workspace created over the API, `stty size` read from inside the pane; then `herdr terminal session control w1:p1 --cols 200 --rows 50` under a 5 s `timeout` — SIGTERM, not SIGKILL — and `stty size` and `pane.list` read again | Born **`40 93`**. After the controller had exited (status 0), `stty size` read **`50 200`** and `scroll.viewport_rows` read **50**. So [#219](#)'s finding is not an artefact of killing the controller and is not one-directional: a clean release leaves the pane where the controller put it, upward as well as downward, and `viewport_rows` follows the PTY exactly as [#84](#) says it does. This is the whole basis of the `once` mode — claim, resize, let go — being useful at all |
| 306 | **Nothing anywhere says whether a desk client is attached, so a node cannot tell the case where a resize sticks from the case where it reverts** | `herdr api schema --json` walked for every key matching `client`/`attach`/`connected`/`observer`; then `herdr status` run against a session with a real TUI client attached and against a headless one, and the two outputs compared | The schema has **two** matches and neither is an answer: `ClientWindowTitleSetParams` and `ClientWindowTitleReason`. `herdr status` is **byte-for-byte identical** in both cases — its `client:` block is the CLI's own version, not a report of anyone attached. Companion to [#221](#)'s "no column count anywhere". **Consequence:** [#19](#) (attached: release restores the desk's geometry) and [#219](#)/[#305](#) (headless: it stays) are opposite outcomes that cannot be predicted before acting, so `pane.size` measures `viewport_rows` *after* releasing and reports which one happened rather than echoing the request — the [#233](#) failure class, refused by construction |

## What a real browser does with the web client (Chromium 1280x900 headless, real node, real herdr pane)

Driven through chromedriver's CDP passthrough so the wheel and key events are **trusted** ones — a
synthetic `WheelEvent` from JS cannot tell a passive listener from a non-passive one, which is the
whole question in [#307](#).

| # | Claim | How | Result |
|---|---|---|---|
| 307 | **A ctrl+wheel over the pane is `preventDefault`ed, so the browser's own page zoom never fires — and Compose renders into a *shadow root*, not into `document`** | real client at `http://127.0.0.1:8799` against a real herdr pane, token seeded into `localStorage`; a bubble-phase `wheel` listener on `window` reading `defaultPrevented`, then `Input.dispatchMouseEvent {type: mouseWheel, modifiers: 2}` over the canvas | **3 of 3 ctrl+wheel events `defaultPrevented`**, `visualViewport.scale` unchanged at **1**, and the pane's own zoom moved **1.3x → 1.8x** with the column readout following `col 1-94` → `col 1-71`. A plain wheel is prevented too (2 of 2), which is `terminalWheel` taking the scroll. So CMP's wheel listener is **non-passive and does call `preventDefault`** — the passive-listener hazard that gated this is not real here. Separately: `document.querySelector('canvas')` finds **nothing**. The canvas lives inside the shadow root `ComposeViewport` builds and carries `tabindex="0"`; any probe or DOM code looking for it has to walk shadow roots |
| 308 | **Standing the offscreen input down is not enough on its own: focus fell to `BODY`, Compose reads keys off its canvas, and a sheet got no keys at all** — Escape-to-dismiss and every sheet shortcut were dead in a browser | the same session: opened the zoom sheet by clicking the real header button, read `activeElement` through the shadow root, sent `=` and `Escape` as trusted key events, then repeated with the canvas focused by hand | With the grid live, focus is the offscreen `DIV[editable]` — as designed. With the sheet open it became **`BODY`**, and `=` did nothing and `Escape` did not dismiss. Focusing the canvas by hand and repeating: `=` moved the zoom **1.8x → 2.0x** and `Escape` closed the sheet. So `focusInput(false)` now *hands* the slot to the canvas rather than merely blurring, and the same run re-measured after the fix reads **`CANVAS`** with `=` moving **2.0x → 2.2x** and Escape dismissing. **No JVM test can see this** — there the sheet's `Modifier.modal` already holds focus, so the whole class of defect is invisible to `runComposeUiTest` |

## Still open

- **Does `pane.read … format=ansi` pass a DCS/APC/SOS/PM payload through?** `skip_sequence` now
  swallows all four, measured against `vte` and against the emulator's own cursor — but whether one
  can *reach* the node through herdr's scrollback read was never established, so the sixel half of
  that bound is a fix without a demonstrated path. *Method: print a sixel and an APC string into a
  throwaway pane, `pane.read source=recent format=ansi`, and look for the payload bytes.*
- **What does herdr do with a grapheme cluster of thousands of marks?** `MAX_CLUSTER_BYTES = 128`
  is asserted faithful against Kampr's own `put`, **not** against a measurement, and the claim that
  herdr opens a new cell for a mark it cannot attach traces to [#226](#), which is about jamo width
  rather than about this. So the *bound* is proved and the *fidelity* is not. *Method: write a base
  plus 5 000 combining marks into a pane and read back where herdr put the next glyph.*
- **Can a `pane.read` row be longer than the pane's width in columns?** This is the precondition for
  the 65 535-column grid `MAX_GRID_CELLS` now defends against — if herdr wraps every row at the PTY
  width before the node ever sees it ([#229](#) says it does for the width walk), the width half of
  that defence is belt to the byte budget's braces. *Method: a very long logical line on a narrow
  pane, `pane.read source=recent`, measure the longest row returned.*
- **Does herdr emit a literal HT in `format=ansi` scrollback?** A tab is one byte and eight columns
  in `column_bound`, an 8x amplification that survives the cluster fix and is now bounded only in
  consequence by `MAX_GRID_CELLS`. If herdr expands tabs before the node reads them, the charge can
  be dropped entirely. *Method: `printf 'a\tb'`, read it back, look for `0x09`.*

- **Whether iTerm2 and WezTerm answer `CSI >0q` with the strings a name table assumes, and whether
  `OSC 1337;File=inline` renders.** Neither is installed here and [#299](#) installed nothing, for
  [#291](#)'s reason — a terminal fetched to be measured is not the terminal an operator has. Both
  keep a name-table row in `kampr-tui`'s `image.rs` so the `Iterm2` variant has a producer, and
  **that row is a guess**: [#299](#) has just shown a name table being wrong about konsole on the
  machine it was written from. The in-band probe runs first and answers for itself, so the guess is
  only ever reached on a terminal that answered nothing.

- **A pty with no emulator behind it answers none of the capability queries, and a fit ladder must
  not read that as a refusal.** [#291](#) measured three real emulators; it did not measure the case
  a CI runner, an ssh peer that does not implement XTWINOPS, or `script -qc … /dev/null` produces —
  where `CSI >0q`, `CSI 14t` and `CSI 16t` all go unanswered because nothing is listening. Observed
  while driving `kampr` under `script`: the query bytes land in the log and no reply ever comes. The
  distinction is load-bearing rather than cosmetic — *the terminal refused* means rung 2 was tried
  and ignored, while *nothing answered* means there was no display size to clamp against, and
  writing `CSI 8;r;c t` in the second case is asking an unbounded display for a window nothing
  constrains ([#291](#) measured konsole doing exactly that when asked). `kampr-tui` reports the two
  as `Refusal::Ignored` and `Refusal::Unmeasured` and never writes the request in the second, which
  is the conservative reading rather than a measured one. *Method: drive the ladder under `script`,
  under an ssh session to a host with no XTWINOPS, and in a CI runner, and record which of the three
  queries answer.*

- **Whether `pane.report_metadata`'s per-source `seq` memory survives a herdr restart, and what an
  *equal* seq does.** [#295](#) measured `seq=5` after `10` dropped and `11` applied; it measured
  neither `seq == last` nor whether the per-source table outlives `server.stop` and a respawn. Both
  decide whether `kampr-core`'s clock-seeded `seq` is load-bearing or merely harmless — a node that
  counted from 1 after a restart would have a whole herd of names dropped and every one answered
  `ok`, which is [#295](#)'s silent-drop with a bigger blast radius. *Method: report at seq N,
  restart that session's herdr, report at N and again at N, `pane.get` after each.*
- **What `pane.process_info` costs per pane per sweep on a real herd.** Naming widened that call from
  agent panes only to **every** pane on the watched sweep, over a socket that is one request per
  connection ([#1](#)). Nothing here is measured and somebody with thirty panes will find it first.
  *Method: a session with 20 panes at `sweep_watched = 3s`, herdr CPU and per-call latency over 60 s,
  against the same run with commands off.*

- **Whether wezterm, foot, xterm or alacritty honour XTWINOPS is unmeasured, and cannot be measured here.** None of the four is installed on this machine and [#291](#) deliberately installed nothing — a terminal fetched to be measured is not the terminal an operator has. What [#291](#) does settle is that the answer is not uniform: two of the three emulators that *are* here ignore `CSI 8;rows;cols t` outright, including the operator's own, so a client cannot assume support and must probe by asking and re-reading. The probe is cheap and self-answering — write the request, re-read `TIOCGWINSZ`, and treat anything past konsole's measured 5 ms as a refusal — so this stays a gap in the table rather than a gap in the code.
- **What ended the codex pane's feeder is still not established ([#268](#)), but three of the four
  candidates are now closed ([#275](#)).** The herdr question the list turned on is answered: an
  alt-screen pane **is** in `layouts[]`, its rect is its full rect and `scroll.viewport_rows` is
  the PTY's own row count — so `resolve_geometry` cannot park on one, and it did not park in any of
  ~200 000 snapshots taken across a background tab, a background workspace, a zoomed sibling, pane
  creation, a split and a herdr restart either. `observe.rs` has nothing to skip: herdr 0.8.2 emits
  one record type with one key set and 2629 of them parsed. `Stop::FrameGap` has no evidence
  behind it: 2610 frames with no sequence gap under a 2 ms producer and across `clear`, alt screen
  in and out, a split and a config reload.

  What is left is the **width prober**, and it is narrower than it looked. `run_observer` holds the
  only `watch::Receiver` for as long as it runs, so `width.changed()` can only error if
  `probe_width` **panicked** — and that arm returns `Stop::ConsumerGone`, which `supervise` treats
  as *nobody is watching this pane any more*: it returns with no log, no backoff and no fault, and
  the entry the registry keeps then has a dead pump behind it. The same arm covers
  `snapshots.changed()`, which cannot error at all while `supervise` holds its `Arc<Inner>`, so
  that half is dead code sharing a name with a live defect. Every path through `observe_cols`,
  `read_width`, `reading` and `rebuilds` was read for a panic and none was found; the door still
  open is a **poisoned `widths` mutex**, which would take every pane at once and the report was one
  pane. Two things would catch the next occurrence in the act, and neither exists yet: telling
  `Stop::ConsumerGone` apart from a prober that died, and a `warn!` on the three places `supervise`
  returns silently. What the operator will see today is the wire, not the log —
  `error.stream_unavailable`, *"this pane's frames stopped arriving; reopen it to restart the
  stream"*, on the pane that stalled and on nothing else.

- ~~A client that reconnects gets no withdrawal, because the node has no memory of what it is
  holding.~~ **Answered by the page rather than by the node ([#264](#)):** `convo` carries an
  additive `fresh`, because a node that restarts cannot remember what any client holds however the
  memory is keyed. What is *not* closed is an older phone, which ignores the field and merges — the
  additive rule buys forward compatibility, not a retrofit.
- ~~A relayed pane hands a joining watcher a grid and a scrollback, and no conversation.~~
  **Fixed ([#263](#)):** every joiner wanting the transcript re-asks the peer, and the hub marks
  the first page it hands each client. The hub still shadows no conversation, so the page costs one
  crossing of the WAN per joiner — cheap because it is the same ask a first watcher already makes,
  and worth re-measuring if a pane ever gets many simultaneous viewers.
- **`a_turn_in_progress_streams_off_the_screen_and_yields_to_the_record` is load-sensitive.** One
  failure in a full-workspace run, then 3 of 3 alone and green in every full run since. `Watch::
  observe` emits a fresh `Change::Show` whenever the previewed text *advances*, so a pane repaint
  racing the appended record produces a second non-empty preview and the test asserts on the first
  one it sees after the append. The mechanism is in the test's window, not in the pump. Left alone
  deliberately; re-run it alone before calling it a regression.

- ~~Does `pane.read recent` with `lines > viewport_rows` scroll a pane with a *detected agent*?~~
  **No — measured, and the interlock has dropped its agent clause (#231).** A live `codex` and a
  live `claude`, both herdr-detected and both holding a ring, answer `lines: 5000` in **1 ms** with
  every row of the ring, `truncated: false`, and `offset_from_bottom`/`max_offset_from_bottom`
  exactly where they were; a pane deliberately marked an agent while running a mouse-mode program
  received no wheel bytes at all, so herdr does not go near the agent’s scroll interface. What *is*
  slow is a live harness whose ring is **empty** — Claude Code clears the scrollback when it takes
  the screen — where every read past `viewport_rows` costs a flat ~375 ms and returns the viewport
  anyway. That is the case `max_offset_from_bottom > 0` already excludes, so it is the only half of
  the interlock left. The premise underneath the old half had also stopped being true: agent panes
  were said to be alt-screen with no ring to miss (#30), and a codex pane read back 402 rows.
- **A real mid-range ARM phone.** #56 is emulator-only; the shaping cost is the part that will move.
- **What a real *phone* browser reports for `hover` and `pointer` ([#266](#)).** The desktop half
  is now measured and closed — [#267](#) is an operator's own desktop answering `[true, false, 10]`,
  which both confirms the media query on real hardware and shows why the touch count could not be
  the question. The phone row is still the specification rather than a measurement, held as a table
  in `BrowserKeyboardTest`; nothing on this machine can be made into one, and letting the harness
  pick the predicate is exactly the defect #266 records.
- ~~Why herdr’s headless rect is one column wider than the PTY it created (#69).~~ **The rect is
  the pane’s outer box and the column it keeps back is the scrollbar’s (#230).** Rendered and read
  off the screen: on a 120-column client the single unbordered pane’s rect is 94 at `x: 26`, its
  content occupies columns 26–118, and column 119 holds `▕` — the scrollbar. Split, each pane
  loses three columns and two rows to its box border plus that same gutter. The gutter is reserved
  whether or not a scrollbar is being drawn, which is why the headless rect is one wider with no
  visible reason.
- ~~How to make the width proof in #211 safe.~~ **Closed.** `pane.read` has no offset and no way
  to ask for the logical lines *of* a set of rows, so the two reads cannot be made one window at
  the API. They are reconciled in the node instead: walked from the bottom, where both are
  anchored on the same row, pairing each logical line with the rows that rebuild it. The
  wide-glyph residual is closed too: a break a double-width glyph made is carried as the pair of
  widths it is and settled against what the pane has already proved, rather than resolved upwards
  on the spot. What cannot be closed is a pane that has never shown a narrow break at all — a
  92-column PTY of wide glyphs and a 93-column one read back byte for byte the same (#220), and
  nothing else on the socket reports a column count (#221) — and that pane is observed at the
  wider of its two candidates, whose extra column no wide glyph could have reached.
- ~~Whether a double-width cell should occupy two cells on the wire or carry a width flag
  (#210).~~ **Answered: two cells in the grid, a per-run `w` on the wire.** The premise of the
  question was wrong — the node's grid was *not* faithful to herdr's cell model. Herdr spends two
  columns on a wide glyph and addresses the next one at col+2, so a grid that spends one disagrees
  with herdr, and that disagreement is the whole of #210. `State::put` now advances by
  `unicode-width` and writes a tail cell, and the wire carries `w` (1 or 2, omitted when 1) rather
  than a sentinel character, so `x` stays exactly the text on screen for copy, find and link
  detection. See `docs/04-wire-protocol.md`.
- ~~**A long interactive prompt can silence the width poll entirely.**~~ **A defect, and fixed —
  but not in the walk’s stop rule (#229).** The ~1900-column line is real and reproduces exactly: a
  1787-column write on a 93-column pane came back as one logical line of **1891** columns over rows
  of 92, eighteen of 93, then the remainder. `rebuilds()` could not reconstruct it because it laid
  every row of a join out at one stride, and one row was a column short — a double-width glyph will
  not straddle the last column, so herdr laid that row out at 92 and the join carries it as 92. The
  rows of a join do not all occupy the same width, so the grid cannot be divided out of the total;
  what the total *does* pin down is the pair the grid must be one of, `p / n` and `p / n + 1`, and
  both are now tried. A grid only one of them rebuilds is proved outright by the rows that filled
  it; a grid both rebuild is exactly #220’s ambiguity. Across 80 live screens at a PTY held to 93
  and 80 more at 92, the old rule measured nothing on **32** of them and the new rule on **3**,
  with no wrong width from either. **Skipping the line was rejected**: a line that cannot be rebuilt
  is a line whose height is unknown, so there is no count of rows to step over with it, and every
  join above it would then be paired with rows that are not its own — which is #211, the defect the
  walk exists to prevent. The stop stays.
- **A dropped keystroke does not say which of two things went wrong.** `KamprConnection.send`
  refuses a keystroke on two different grounds — the socket is not live, which is the deliberate
  rule that a half-typed command must not be replayed into a live shell a reconnect later, and
  `outbox.trySend` failing because the 64-message buffer is full while the socket *is* live, which
  means the writer is suspended in `session.send` behind a stalled link. Both land on
  `noteInput(pane, delivered = false)` and both surface as the same "Not sent · N" badge, so the
  badge cannot distinguish "there is nowhere to put this" from "the writer is behind". Nothing is
  measured here: the second case needs a real stalled link and a run of typing longer than the
  buffer to reach at all, and it has never been observed. Telling them apart needs a reading of
  whether the pump is making progress — the count queued and the age of the oldest entry when the
  refusal happens — which is a state the connection does not keep today. Left alone deliberately:
  the buffer is what bounds the backlog, and an unbounded one would trade a visible refusal for a
  silent delay. If the badge is ever reported from a phone, this is the first thing to instrument.

## What the harnesses write down beside their transcripts, and what Kampr's own arithmetic makes of it (Claude Code 2.1.248 and 2.1.250 on this desktop, nine real transcripts in one project; kampr at 3486986)

| # | Claim | How | Result |
|---|---|---|---|
| 309 | **Claude Code no longer writes sidechain records into the main transcript at all — subagents moved to their own files under a per-session directory, and `ClaudeParser`'s `is_sidechain` skip is now dead code against every current version** | `jq -r '.type' <transcript> \| sort \| uniq -c` and `jq 'select(.isSidechain==true)' \| wc -l` over `~/.claude/projects/-home-dbrain-dev-kampr/*.jsonl`; then `find` over the sibling directories | **Zero** sidechain records in the main transcript of a 2.1.248 session that ran four subagents. They are at `~/.claude/projects/<slug>/<session-uuid>/subagents/agent-<agentId>.jsonl`, one file per agent, each with a sibling `agent-<agentId>.meta.json`. Two more things live in that directory: **`tool-results/<id>.txt`**, which is where a large tool result is offloaded to, and **`custom-title.json`** = `{"customTitle":"kampr-cli"}`. The subagent files use the **same record grammar as the main transcript** — `type`/`uuid`/`message.content` blocks — so `ClaudeParser` reads one unchanged and no second parser is needed |
| 310 | **The parent transcript names the subagent file exactly, so nothing has to be guessed or matched by time** | `jq -c 'select(.toolUseResult.agentId != null) \| .toolUseResult'` over the same transcript, against the `subagents/` listing | The `Agent` tool's `toolUseResult` is `{agentId, description, isAsync, outputFile, resolvedModel, status, canReadOutputFile}` — e.g. `{"agentId":"a3a91080ac1e83a12", "description":"Map the manage op end-to-end path", "isAsync":true, "outputFile":"/tmp/claude-1000/…/tasks/<agentId>.output", "resolvedModel":"claude-opus-5[1m]", "status":"async_launched"}` — and `agentId` **is** the filename. The sibling meta is `{"agentType":"Explore","description":"…","toolUseId":"toolu_…","spawnDepth":1}`, so the agent's kind and its one-line description are available without parsing a single turn. `spawnDepth` proves a subagent can spawn its own, so anything built on this must nest rather than assume one level. Completion arrives back in the parent as a **`queue-operation`** record whose `content` is a `<task-notification>` block carrying `<status>completed</status>` and the agent's whole result — which is a shape worth knowing before anything parses it |
| 311 | **`~/.claude/sessions/<pid>.json` exists from the moment a session opens, minutes before the transcript does — so a pane can be known to be running an agent, and *which* session, before there is any transcript to find** | `ls -la ~/.claude/sessions/`, `jq` over a live marker, and `stat -c %w` on the transcript against the marker's own `startedAt` | The marker for this session was written at **13:00:13** and the transcript file was not created until **13:02:55** — 2 min 42 s later, at the first submit, with the prompt on **line 6** (lines 1-5 are `mode`, `permission-mode`, `atis-latch`, `bridge-session`, `file-history-snapshot`). The marker carries `{pid, sessionId, cwd, startedAt, procStart, version, peerProtocol, peerFeatures, kind, entrypoint, pidDomain, messagingSocketPath, name, nameSource, nameSince, status, updatedAt, statusUpdatedAt, bridgeSessionId}`. `procStart` is field 22 of `/proc/<pid>/stat` verbatim, which is what `ClaudeAdapter::locate_by_process` already checks. **Two things here are read but not measured**: `status` was observed as `"busy"` on a working session and `"idle"` on others, but nothing watched one across a whole turn, so whether it tracks or is stamped once is **not established**; and `messagingSocketPath` (`/run/user/1000/cc-socks/<pid>.sock`, advertised with `peerFeatures: ["notify_idle", …]`) was **not probed at all** — its existence is measured, everything about what it speaks is a guess |
| 312 | **The record types Kampr's Claude parser drops, counted — it handles `assistant` and `user` and silently discards fourteen other kinds, several of which are things a power user would want on screen** | `cat *.jsonl \| jq -r '.type'` and `jq -r 'select(.type=="attachment") \| .attachment.type'` and `jq -r 'select(.type=="system") \| .subtype'`, summed over nine transcripts in one project | Dropped, with counts: `ai-title` 38 (a generated conversation **title**, beside `custom-title.json` for a manual one) - `agent-name` 9 - `system/turn_duration` **413** (`durationMs` + `messageCount` per turn — real timings, not inferred) - `system/compact_boundary` 7 (`preTokens`, `postTokens`, `cumulativeDroppedTokens`, `trigger`) - `queue-operation` 16 (**queued prompts**, enqueue/remove with a `reason` such as `absorbed_mid_turn`) - `permission-mode` and `mode` 37 each (`plan`/`bypassPermissions`/`normal`) - `last-prompt` 36 - `file-history-snapshot` / `file-history-delta` 5 each (per-file backup history keyed by `messageId`, with `trackingPath` — Claude's own undo, i.e. "what did this file look like before that turn" without git) - `cost-state` - `frame-link` - `artifact-comment-monitor` / `artifact-autoreact-ledger`. Attachment subtypes: `total_tokens_reminder` **3605** (noise, ignore) - `queued_command` 106 - `edited_text_file` 35 - `file` 22 - `deferred_tools_delta` 17 - `agent_listing_delta` 16 - `auto_mode` 15 - `date_change` 14 - `compact_file_reference` 12 - `skill_listing` 9 - `invoked_skills` 6 - `hook_system_message` 5 - `plan_file_reference` 4 - `command_permissions` 3 - `plan_mode` / `plan_mode_exit` 2 each - `inlined_image_paths` 1. Assistant records also carry **`effort`** (262) and a human-readable **`slug`** (172). **This census is Claude only** — nothing equivalent has been run against a Codex rollout or an agy transcript, so any shape built on it must be per-adapter and wholly optional |
| 313 | **A pane's real foreground job is reachable from procfs even in the case `pane.process_info` gives up on — which is every interactive shell on this machine (#297), and therefore every pane whose name the sidebar cannot show** | For every `bash` on the machine: `cat /proc/<pid>/task/*/children`, then `/proc/<child>/comm` and field 5 (pgid) of `/proc/<child>/stat` against field 8 (tpgid) of the shell's | The walk names the job in exactly the case that defeats `ProcessInfo::command`: rows such as `bash 2140605 -> 2140606 = claude, pgid=2140605` — the child's process group **equal to the shell's**, which is the condition `model.rs:157` returns `None` for. `claude`, `ssh` and `herdr` were all named this way under shells where herdr reports only `bash`. So `PaneEntry.cmd`/`.argv` and the naming template's `[ ({argv\|cmd})]` were never the missing half; the value was. **Not measured**: a backgrounded job, a multi-member pipeline, and — the one that matters — whether a walk can name a job that has *already exited*, which would be worse than naming none. Re-walk per sweep and never cache is the rule until that is measured |
| 314 | **Kampr's own page arithmetic loses the operator's question: every tool call is a turn, so one prompt and the reply to it is far more turns than a page holds, and the page opens headless** | Drove `ClaudeAdapter::open_path` + `Journal::poll` + `page_before(None, 40)` over a live transcript of this very session and counted turns between the first `Role::User` turn and the next | **One prompt and the reply to it parsed to 53 turns** against `convo::PAGE` of 40, so a page taken from the newest turn opened 13 turns *into* the reply and the question that caused it was not in it — on a brand-new session that had been asked exactly one thing, on a view pinned to its own end with nothing above to scroll to. `Exchange.kt`'s `Reply` already anticipates the shape (*"a reply with nothing in front of it is a reply, not a broken exchange"*) and renders it headless, which is right for a genuinely older page and wrong for the first thing a reader sees. Fixed by making the page reach back to the question that opens the reply it landed in, bounded, with `more` left to say when the bound was hit. **Not the same defect as the `fresh` page**: `convo::Held` lives in the per-socket session, so a reconnect really does replace a conversation with the newest 40 — that is real, and it is not what was reported |
| 315 | **The Android notification icon rendered as a blob because its artwork occupied a third of its canvas and sat below centre, and centring it is not enough — a face does not survive the reduction at any offset** | Rasterised `ic_kampr_notification.xml` through `rsvg-convert` at 512 px and at the 24 px mdpi actually draws, and trimmed the ink box with ImageMagick; then the same for a keylined variant and for twelve candidate replacements | The sheep's ink box was **262x182 at +125+244** of a 512 viewport — 51% of the width, **35% of the height**, ink centre 79 units (15%) below the canvas centre — which is **12x9 px at +6+11** at mdpi: a nine-row blob in the bottom half of a twenty-four-row badge. Its interior could not exist at that size either: the eyes are 34x36 viewport units and the muzzle 60x46, i.e. **1.6x1.7 px and 2.8x2.2 px**. Scaling it 1.717x about its ink centre and lifting it 79 units does put it on the keyline (450x314, 22x16 px) and the eyes are still 1.6 px, so the result is a *legible* blob rather than an illegible one. Replaced with the tent from the same launcher artwork: ink **470x378, centred on both axes, 22x18 px at mdpi**. Measured across the twelve candidates: **a door taller than about a third of the body splits the silhouette into two legs and the glyph reads as a caret**, not a tent. The file's own comment had said *"It has to read at 24dp in a status bar"* since it was written; nothing had ever checked it |
| 316 | **The reply box clears a selection handle by one dp on a rotated pane, and the Herd top inset is single-counted on a phone but doubled on the Desktop breakpoint** | Composed the real `ConversationView` and `HerdPortrait` through `screenInset`/`keyboardInset` in `ComposerInsetTest`'s own harness and read `getUnclippedBoundsInRoot`, at three postures and with `safe.top = 48.dp` | A Compose selection handle hangs **25 dp** below the line it belongs to. Clearance under the reply field measured **116 dp** portrait with a tab bar, **50 dp** portrait without, and **26 dp** on the rotated pane — one dp of slack, on the posture that already needed its own test. The left edge is the same shape: the field starts at **28 dp** and a start handle hangs 25 dp to its left, leaving 3 dp. Separately, `HerdPortrait`'s title lands at **67 dp** under a 48 dp inset — one inset plus its own 16 dp, correctly single-counted, so the phone's reported whole-window offset is **not** in the padding arithmetic. `HerdSidebar` **is** doubled: `KamprApp.kt:304` applies `screenInset` and `HerdScreen.kt:227` adds `18.dp + safe.top` again inside it, which is two status bars above the sidebar title on every non-pane screen at the Desktop breakpoint. **Unmeasured**: why a real phone's handles land off screen at all. Compose draws them in a `Popup`, which is a real window, and `runComposeUiTest` creates none — so nothing in this suite can see one, and the theme setting opaque `statusBarColor`/`navigationBarColor` against `enableEdgeToEdge()` is a candidate, not a finding |

## What the procfs walk and the client harnesses turned up while being built (this desktop; synthetic `/proc` trees and real child processes; `Medium_Phone_API_35` emulator, API 35)

| # | Claim | How | Result |
|---|---|---|---|
| 317 | **A killed child stays in its parent's `children` file until it is reaped, so the stale job [#313](#) warned about is real — and an empty `cmdline` is the only thing that tells it from a live one** | A real child killed under a parent that had `exec`'d and therefore never reaps it, with `/proc/<parent>/task/*/children` and the child's `stat`/`cmdline` read afterwards | The kernel **still lists the dead pid** in `children`, state `Z`, and every other field still reads plausibly — so a walk that trusted the listing would name a job that had already exited, which is worse than naming none. `/proc/<pid>/cmdline` is **empty** for a zombie and is what the walk keys on. Two more things the same walk surfaced, both of which W8 *creates* by making these panes nameable at all: a harness launched with a large `--append-system-prompt` has a **multi-kilobyte, newline-bearing command line** — which the default naming template would render straight into a pane's name, into every herd patch, and back into herdr's own pane title — so `{argv}` is now folded and capped at 256 characters; and where one shell wraps another (`bash -> bash <script> -> claude`, which is this machine's own `llm-review`), the walk must return **nearest-to-the-shell first**, or an agent that spawns an agent leaves two markers under one shell and the wrong one wins |
| 318 | **A vector drawable's geometry can be asserted on-device to the pixel, and two independent rasterisers agree — but only both halves of the assertion catch both defects** | `ic_kampr_notification.xml` inflated through a `createConfigurationContext` pinned to `DENSITY_MEDIUM`, drawn into a 24x24 `ARGB_8888` bitmap and scanned for non-zero alpha; cross-checked against `rsvg-convert -w 24 -h 24` measured in PIL; then two mutants run on a real emulator | Skia on-device and `rsvg-convert` returned **exactly the same ink boxes** — 22x18 at +1+3 for the shipped tent, 14x9 at +5+11 for the old sheep — so a 1 px tolerance on a 24 px raster is the raster's own granularity and not renderer disagreement. **The two assertions catch different defects and both are needed**: the old sheep is *horizontally* centred (margins 6/6), so a centring-only test passes it and only the keyline check catches it; while the tent wrapped in `translateY="43"` is still 22 wide, so only the centring check catches that. **An emulator trap worth an hour**: an AVD with a secure lock credential boots headless to `RUNNING_LOCKED`, has no `/sdcard`, and `am instrument` dies `SecurityException: Package … is not encryption aware` — after which `connectedAndroidTest` **hangs for a year**, because AGP passes `testTimeoutSeconds 31536000`. `wm dismiss-keyguard`, `locksettings set-disabled true` and a reboot all failed to clear it; an AVD with no lock credential works |
| 319 | **The rotated pane's reply box is only fixed for the one keyboard height it was measured against — a taller one collapses it to nothing** | The real `ConversationView` composed in `ComposerInsetTest`'s harness at `ROTATED_WINDOW`, sweeping `ime` past the 212 dp the existing test uses | At **`ime = 280.dp`** the reply field measures **0 dp tall** (bounds 105..105): the column overflows and the box the operator types into is gone, while `aRotatedPaneWithTheKeysUpShowsTheWholeReplyBox` goes on passing because it only ever asks about 212 dp. Not a regression — it has presumably always been so — and not fixed here, because the fix is a layout decision rather than a number. Measured alongside it: the clearance under the reply field is **116 / 50 / 26 dp** at the three postures against the **25 dp** a Compose selection handle hangs below its line, and **28 dp** to the left of the field's text start against the same 25 dp. Both now hold as named constraints rather than as the sum of two paddings chosen for looks, but the rotated margin is one dp and a vendor handle drawable a fraction larger would breach it |
| 320 | **A Claude prompt queue is worked by four operations, not the two [#312](#) named — folding only `enqueue`/`remove` leaves it permanently full** | `jq -r 'select(.type=="queue-operation")\|.operation' *.jsonl \| sort \| uniq -c` over every transcript of one project, then the same with `.reason` and whether `.content` is null | `enqueue` **813** · `remove` **543** (no reason, or `absorbed_mid_turn`, or `delivered_to_agent`) · **`dequeue` 253, with `content` null in every one** · **`popAll` 3**, whose content is the whole queue joined. An ordinary delivery leaves a **`dequeue`**, not a `remove` — so a fold that knows only the two operations [#312](#) listed left **141** prompts standing on a session that had finished every one of them, and folding all four leaves nought. Because `dequeue` carries no content, the head has to be taken by position rather than matched by text. [#312](#)'s census is otherwise unchanged; this supersedes only its parenthetical about that one record type |
| 321 | **[#319](#)'s reply box had two things squeezing it, and fixing either alone leaves the other — the pane header takes the room first, and then the column serves the reply box last** | Swept `ime` through 212/240/280/320 dp on `ROTATED_WINDOW` driving the **real** `PaneScreenMobile` rather than a fixed top padding, then reverted each half in turn | The header floats over the conversation and is paid for as a top padding, so on a 411 dp rotated window with a 280 dp keyboard it held **83 of the 131 dp** below the keys and left 48 — of which the composer's own paddings are 48, so the field measured **0**. Capping the header at what leaves `REPLY_ROOM = 70 dp` fixes that case. It does **not** fix the second: a column measures its unweighted children in index order, so with the bar present the reply box is served last and got **56 dp of 70**. Both reverts were run: the header's alone fails the 280 dp sweep, the column's alone fails a direct layout test. **The second is invisible from most postures** — the bar is composed only while the keyboard is *closed*, so the two compete only when the operator opens search and the keys then rise, and a harness that pads by a constant instead of driving the real pane screen passes with the header's half reverted ([#191](#) again). Driving that posture through the search field hangs: focus lands in a text field and flipping the IME under it leaves `waitForIdle` spinning, so the ordering is asserted at the layout with children of the measured heights |
| 322 | **The per-harness census [#312](#) and [#320](#) owed: Codex records two of Kampr's five facets *better* than Claude does and one not at all, and agy records almost nothing** | `jq` over 11 real Codex rollouts (361 MB, `~/.codex/sessions`) and 26 agy transcripts (`~/.gemini/antigravity-cli/brain/*/.system_generated/logs/`), counting record types and then reading the payloads of every candidate | **Codex.** Record types are `response_item` 7731 · `event_msg` 5154 · `turn_context` 129 · `compacted` 12 · `session_meta` 11 · `world_state` 8, with `event_msg` subtypes `token_count` 2018 · `exec_command_end` 1354 · `agent_message` 886 · `patch_apply_end` 399 · `user_message` 124 · `task_started`/`task_complete` 118 each · `context_compacted` 12. **Timings are stronger than Claude's**: `task_complete` carries `duration_ms` outright, beside `started_at`, `completed_at`, `time_to_first_token_ms` and a `turn_id`, where Claude's `turn_duration` has to be matched to a turn by `parentUuid`. **Mode is real but on different axes**: `turn_context.payload` carries `approval_policy` (`never`), `collaboration_mode`, `sandbox_policy` and `permission_profile` — none of which is Claude's `normal\|plan\|bypassPermissions`, so they map onto the same *field* and must not be assumed to mean the same thing. **Compaction is position-only**: `context_compacted`'s whole payload is `{"type":"context_compacted"}` and the `compacted` record carries `replacement_history` but no token counts, so where Claude says 756165 → 18709 Codex can only say *here*. **There is no title and no queue** — the 2700 `payload.name` hits are tool-call names, not session names. **agy** is `PLANNER_RESPONSE`/`GENERIC`/`SYSTEM_MESSAGE`/`USER_INPUT`/`CHECKPOINT` with `{type, step_index, status, source, created_at}`: one `CHECKPOINT` per session is a compaction-shaped boundary, and nothing else lands. Its `created_at` deltas are **deliberately not** a timing facet — the gap between two steps contains the operator's own thinking time, which is a different fact from a duration the harness recorded, and filling a facet from a field that merely reads like one is the trap this census exists to avoid |
| 323 | **A subagent transcript is *entirely* sidechain records, so the parser that correctly drops them in a pane's own transcript returned nothing at all for a launched one — and the fixtures could not show it, because they were built without the field** | Parsed a real `subagents/agent-*.jsonl` (`d3c683a3…`, 134 records) through `ClaudeAdapter`, before and after; `jq -c '{type,isSidechain}'` over that file and over the test fixtures beside it | The real file sets **`isSidechain: true` on 134 of 134 records**; the fixtures written for the subagent tests set it **null on every one**. `ClaudeParser` drops sidechain records — correctly, in a pane's own transcript, where the flag marks words belonging to something the agent *launched* and inlining them would put a subagent's voice in the parent's. In the launched conversation's own file the flag marks every record there is, so the same rule emptied it: the real transcript parsed to **0 turns**, and would have shipped as a panel that opens and shows nothing. Scoped to the boundary it belongs at — `open_sub` builds a parser that keeps them, `open_path` still drops them — the same file parses to **58 turns**. **The fixture is the finding.** Eight tests passed against data that differed from the real thing in exactly the field the code branches on, which is [#191](#) in a new place: a harness built to pass rather than built from a measurement cannot fail for the reason that matters. Fixtures for a harness's own files are now built from a `jq` of a real one |

## Starting herdr from a node that finds it stopped (herdr 0.8.2 on this desktop, in throwaway `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` roots; kampr at 5f73245)

Every row below was measured in a config root of its own, so the operator's real default server was
never a participant. `research/probe/default-session.sh` is the script.

| # | Claim | How | Result |
|---|---|---|---|
| 324 | **`herdr server --session default` *is* the default session, not a named one** — the name is an alias, so a node has one spelling that starts either kind of server | A cold root (`XDG_CONFIG_HOME=$tmp`, `onboarding = false`, nothing running): `herdr server --session default`, then `herdr session list --json` and an `ls` of the config root | It binds **`$tmp/herdr/herdr.sock`** — the default socket — and is listed as the `"default":true` entry. **No `sessions/default/` directory is created at all**; the same command with any other name creates `sessions/<name>/herdr.sock` and a second, separate entry. So `session.create` naming `default` starts the default server rather than a namesake beside it, and one spawn path serves both |
| 325 | **A second `herdr server` for a default already running exits 1 and changes nothing** — [#243](#)'s result for named sessions, now measured for the default | A bare `herdr server` in the foreground of a root whose default server was already up, then `session list --json`, the socket's file type, and `kill -0` on the first server | Exit **1**, stderr `error: herdr server is already running` followed by the `api socket:` path. The running server is untouched, still listed, still owns a live socket. `herdr server --session default` against the same running default says exactly the same thing. **So a node that races itself, or races the operator, loses harmlessly** — which is what lets the wake be attempted rather than negotiated |
| 326 | **A herdr becomes listed, then connectable, then able to answer — and the three are ~50 ms apart, but a caller in the gap is made to wait rather than refused** | The cold root of [#324](#), spawned detached and watched at 2 ms resolution three ways: `herdr session list --json` polled for `"running":true`; the socket file; and a real `connect()` + `session.snapshot` taking the first *result*. Then, separately, a connection opened the instant it was accepted, with the call issued on it immediately and the reply timed (`research/probe/default-session.sh`, `herdr-ready.py`, three runs each) | Before any server exists the list already carries `{"default":true,"name":"default","running":false}`, `socket_path` and all. After the spawn: **listed running 1-3 ms**, **socket file and `connect()` 1-2 ms**, **first answered call 50/51/54 ms**. The early connection is **held open and answered at 51/53/51 ms** — not closed, not refused. So the ~50 ms is startup the caller waits through, **not** a window in which calls fail: the failure mode a wake has to avoid is not a lying socket but the ~1 ms before the socket exists at all, when a call fails outright. Readiness is therefore an answered call — which is the same wait the op was going to do anyway |

## The pane's own composer, and what empties it (herdr 0.8.2 on this desktop, throwaway `kampr-probe-composer-<pid>` sessions, 95x40)

**These four were proposed unnumbered and assigned 327-330 by the writer**, which is the rule at
the top of this file working as intended — the log is append-only and its numbers are permanent
identifiers that code cites, so a task appending from the side proposes and one writer assigns,
after 77/78/92 were each landed twice by parallel work. `crates/kampr-journal/src/composer.rs` and
the three harness modules cite them by description rather than by number.

Driven by `research/probe/composer-line.py`, which stands up a herdr of its own, creates a
workspace per harness, runs the agent in it, and reads the pane back through a real
`herdr terminal session observe --cols 95 --rows 40` folded into `research/probe/vt.py` — so the
caret is herdr's own and not one the probe chose. Every screen quoted is in
`crates/kampr-journal/tests/fixtures/composer/`. Torn down afterwards; the operator's default
server never took part.

| # | Claim | How | Result |
|---|---|---|---|
| 327 | **All three harnesses paint the composer the same way — a marker, one separator cell, then the text from column 2 — but Claude's separator is a NON-BREAKING SPACE, so a matcher that knows only `' '` does not recognise its composer at all the moment anything is typed into it** | `pane.send_text` of `the quick brown fox` into a live `claude` 2.1.250, `codex` 0.149.1 and `agy` 1.1.22, each read back off its own observe stream | Claude `'\u276f\u00a0the quick brown fox'`, Codex `'\u203a the quick brown fox'`, agy `'> the quick brown fox'` — **caret column 21 in all three**, i.e. input starts at **column 2** everywhere. Claude's U+00A0 is the odd one and it is load-bearing: `live::opened_by` requires `' '` or end-of-line, so `live::read`'s `rposition` finds **no** composer row on a Claude screen with anything typed in it. That is benign for the live preview — the walk falls through to `screen.len()` and skips the row as an unindented boundary while its body is still empty — but it is why `composer.rs` carries a matcher of its own rather than reusing that one. A 200-column entry **wraps onto rows indented by exactly 2** on all three, with the caret on the last of them |
| 328 | **An empty composer is not an empty line: Claude and Codex paint a placeholder into the very cells the operator's words would occupy, so nothing in the text separates a box somebody is typing in from one the harness is advertising in — the caret is the only discriminator** | The same three panes read with the composer empty, sampled four times 2.5 s apart, and again across three separate runs | Claude paints a **rotating** hint — `Try "refactor <filepath>"`, `Try "create a util logging.py that..."`, `Try "write a test for <filepath>"`, `Try "fix typecheck errors"` — one per session, **stable within a session** across all four samples, different across runs, so the set is not enumerable and must not be matched on. Codex paints a fixed `Ask Codex to do anything`. agy paints a bare `>`. In every empty case the **caret is at column 2**, and with text it is at 2 + the length. **The measured limitation:** `ctrl+a` also leaves the caret at column 2 with the line still full, on all three — captured as `*-caret-at-home.txt` — and nothing else on screen tells the two apart, so a reader keyed on the caret reports *nothing* for that state. Absent, not wrong, is the failure this is allowed to have |
| 329 | **The keystroke that clears a composer is DIFFERENT ON EVERY HARNESS, and each harness's wrong key fails in its own way — one silently deletes part of the sentence, the other quits the agent** | A 200-column entry wrapped over three rows in each harness, then one send of each candidate, counting rows still carrying the text and reading the caret back (`composer-line.py`, the *what clears it* block) | **`ctrl+u` (`\x15`) clears Codex's and agy's whole buffer in one send, wrapped or not — and takes exactly ONE VISUAL ROW of Claude's**: 3 rows became 2, and eight sends were needed to empty it, a count that is a function of the entry's length and therefore a guess for any other. `ctrl+a ctrl+k` behaves the same way on Claude (one row; the caret moved to the row above), and clears Codex and agy whole. **`ctrl+c` (`\x03`) clears Claude's and Codex's whole buffer in one send and leaves both running — and on agy clears nothing at all and paints `press ctrl+c again to exit`.** `esc` moves nothing on any of the three. 200 backspaces clear Claude and Codex and leave 2 of agy's 3 rows, so a burst is not a general answer either. So the measured single-send clear is **claude → `ctrl+c`, codex → `ctrl+u`, agy → `ctrl+u`**, which is what the three `CLEAR` constants carry. **Undo:** `ctrl+u` followed by `ctrl+y` restored the text verbatim on all three, and Claude paints `Ctrl+Y to paste deleted text` above the box while it is available; whether Claude's `ctrl+c` clear is `ctrl+y`-undoable was **not** measured |
| 330 | **The concatenation being reported is real and is `pane.send_text` doing exactly what it says: two sends with no submit between them arrive as one line** | `pane.send_text` of `half a sentence ` then `and the rest`, no carriage return, on all three harnesses | The composer row reads `half a sentence and the rest` on every one. This is [#9](#) seen from the client's end: `input` appends, nothing in Kampr cleared the line first, and a reply sent from a phone therefore submits joined to whatever the desk had left there. Nothing on the phone had ever shown the first half, which is the part that made it surprising rather than merely occasionally wrong |

## Whether anything can tell that a command is waiting for input (this desktop, kernel 7.1.8-1-cachyos, yama `ptrace_scope=1`, `kptr_restrict=0`; herdr 0.8.2 in a throwaway `fleetprobe` session, torn down; `research/probe/waiting-on-input.py` and `research/probe/fleet-supervisor.py`)

Asked for the fleet-run board, which needs to know that `pacman -Syu` on one host has stopped and
is asking something. Two readings of the same question: from **outside** the pane, the way the node
sees a herdr pane today, and from a **supervisor that owns the pty**. They do not agree, and the
outside reading is the one that fails.

| # | Claim | How | Result |
|---|---|---|---|
| 331 | **The node cannot see whether a herdr pane's job is blocked on a read. `/proc/<pid>/syscall` is EPERM to a same-user reader that is not an ancestor**, which the node never is — herdr forks the panes, and herdr is its own service | A `fleetprobe` session's pane driven through `pane.send_text`, sampled by a sibling process (`is_ancestor` printed `NO`) reading `stat`, `wchan`, `syscall` and `fd/0` for every pid below the shell | `syscall` is `<EPERM 1>` for **every** same-user pid sampled, at the prompt and under a job alike. `wchan` **is** readable and does separate the states — `do_select` at a ble.sh prompt, `wait_woken` under `cat` and under `read -p`, `hrtimer_nanosleep` under `sleep` — but it is a kernel symbol name with no stable contract, and [#332](#) removes it for the case that matters. The outside reading is therefore **not** the basis for a waiting signal |
| 332 | **A job running as root denies the node everything at once**: `syscall`, `wchan` and `fd/0` are all refused to an unprivileged reader, so the node can see literally nothing about the state of `sudo pacman` — the exact command the fleet board exists to run | `sudo pacman -S bash` in the probe pane, sampled from the unprivileged sibling | `syscall=<EPERM 13>`, `wchan=0`, `fd0=<errno 13>` for the `sudo` and `pacman` pids both. Not degraded — blank. A ladder that reads "unknown" here would report *unknown* for every privileged fleet command ever run, which is the whole use case |
| 333 | **ECHO is not a password signal on a shell pane: ble.sh leaves the tty with ECHO OFF at an ordinary prompt**, so the one termios bit that looks definitive is already off before anything asks for a secret | `tcgetattr` on the pane's own `/dev/pts/N`, opened `O_RDONLY\|O_NOCTTY`, with the shell sitting idle at its prompt | `ECHO OFF` at the bare prompt, `ECHO on` the moment a job takes the tty. Exactly inverted from the assumption. termios is readable from outside — that part works — but on a pane with ble.sh on it the bit means nothing. See [#297](#) for the other place ble.sh invalidated an assumption about the shell |
| 334 | **A supervisor that is the job's own parent *and shares its uid* reads `syscall` fine, and it separates waiting from silent work from CPU work** — which is the whole ladder, available only from inside | `fleet-supervisor.py` spawning each case on a pty it owns, run twice: as the operator and under `sudo` | Waiting on a prompt: `syscall='0 0x0 …'` — `read(2)`, **fd 0**. `sleep 5`: `230` (`clock_nanosleep`) — not a read, correctly **not** waiting. Busy loop: `running`, state `R`. As root, `sudo pacman -S bash` at its prompt reads `0 0x0 …` on the `pacman` pid — **the case [#332](#) could not see at all**. The privilege half is load-bearing and not optional: an unprivileged parent gets `<EPERM 13>` for its *own* child once that child is setuid `sudo`, so the supervisor must be `sudo kampr-fleet-exec -- pacman`, never `kampr-fleet-exec -- sudo pacman` |
| 335 | **`cat` is parked in `splice(2)`, not `read(2)`, so a check keyed on the read number alone calls a waiting job busy** | The same supervisor, `cat` with nothing on its stdin | `syscall='275 0x0 0x0 0x4 …'` — 275 is `splice`, and its **first argument is still fd 0**. So the test that holds is *a read-family call whose fd argument is 0*, not `nr == 0`. Coreutils picks the syscall; the fd is the invariant |
| 336 | **What `pacman` actually writes at its prompt**, measured rather than assumed, and what the cursor does around it | `sudo pacman -S bash` on a supervisor-owned pty, read back raw, then answered `n` | The line is `:: Proceed with installation? [Y/n] ` followed by `\x1b[?25h` — **it shows the cursor as it asks**, and hides it again (`\x1b[?25l`) the moment the answer lands. The preamble is `warning: bash-5.3.15-2 is up to date -- reinstalling`. `sudo` also emits an OSC **3008** audit record (`;user=…;targetuser=root;type=session`) around the run, which is noise for our purposes but will reach the emulator. **Not measured:** the replace/conflict prompts, which need a real conflict to produce |
| 337 | **The supervisor does not have to scrape for the exit code or for a password prompt — it gets both directly**, and on a pty with no shell on it the termios bit [#333](#) ruined is honest again | `wait(2)` on three known exits; `getpass.getpass()` on a supervisor-owned pty | `true`→0, `false`→1, `sh -c 'exit 42'`→42, all exact from `wait`. And a password prompt on a bare pty reads **`ECHO OFF` with the job parked in `read(2)` on fd 0** — the two signals together, with no ble.sh in between to confuse the first. So "this is a secret, mask the box" is a measurement here even though [#333](#) says it never can be on a shell pane |

## How a privileged supervisor reports back, and what survives the privilege wall (this desktop, sudo 1.9.x, kernel 7.1.8-1-cachyos)

[#332](#) and [#334](#) leave one gap: the command the fleet board exists to run is `sudo pacman`,
and a supervisor at the operator's own privilege is blind to it. The fix is a helper that runs
*under* the escalation rather than beside it — which needs a channel back to the node that survives
`sudo`, and a check on who is on the other end of it.

| # | Claim | How | Result |
|---|---|---|---|
| 338 | **`sudo` closes every descriptor above 2, so a privileged helper cannot be handed one — but it can connect *out* to a unix socket, and root connects to a `0600` socket owned by the operator** | `dup2` of a socketpair onto fd 3 across `exec`, with and without `sudo`; then a `0600` socket in a `0700` directory, connected to by `sudo -n python3` | Plain child: **`SURVIVED`**. Through `sudo`: **`CLOSED`** — `closefrom` is on by default and the node cannot pass an fd through an escalation. The socket works the other way round: root connected to the operator's `0600` socket and `SO_PEERCRED` on the accepted connection read back **`uid=0`** with the connecting pid, so the node can *verify* the helper rather than trust whatever appears. The report channel is therefore a connect-out socket in a `0700` directory, mode `0600`, checked with `SO_PEERCRED`. **The mode is load-bearing**: bound under the ordinary umask the socket is `0755`, and any local user could then feed the node invented states for somebody else's fleet run |
| 339 | **ECHO on a pty the node owns stays readable even for a setuid child whose `/proc` is closed to it — so "this is asking for a secret" is the one piece of state that survives the privilege wall** | `su -` on a supervisor-owned pty, sampled from the unprivileged parent | `/proc/<su>/syscall` is `<errno 13>` as [#332](#) predicts, and the pty reads **`ECHO OFF`** with `Password:` on it. termios belongs to the tty and the node holds the master, so the escalation cannot hide it. This is the rung that lets a `sudo` prompt for its own password be shown as a question rather than as [#331](#)'s shrug — and it is not in tension with [#333](#), which is about a pane with a *shell* on it, not a pty holding one command |
| 340 | **ECHO alone does not mean a secret — `vim` and `less` turn it off too. What separates a password prompt from a full-screen program is ICANON**, and the two together are the whole test | `tcgetattr` on a supervisor-owned pty under `su -`, `getpass`, an ordinary `read` prompt, `vim -u NONE`, `less` and `top` | Password prompts (`su`, `getpass`): **ECHO off, ICANON on**. An ordinary prompt: ECHO on, ICANON on. `vim` and `less`: **ECHO off, ICANON OFF** — they are in raw mode, not asking for a secret. `top` touches neither. So `secret = !ECHO && ICANON`, and `!ICANON` is its own thing: a program painting the whole screen, for which the unterminated tail is not a prompt and must not be shown as one. Testing ECHO alone marks every full-screen program a fleet run ever starts as a password |
| 341 | **`sudo` puts the outer terminal into RAW MODE — but only when the command it runs has a controlling terminal, which a fleet run always gives it. So under `sudo` the pty's termios describes sudo's relay and not the command**, and both [#339](#)'s and [#340](#)'s readings are about the wrong process | The same `sudo -n sh -c 'printf "Proceed? [Y/n] "; read a'` on a supervisor-owned pty, run twice: with the child `setsid` only, and with `setsid` + `TIOCSCTTY`. sudo 1.9.17p2 | Without a controlling terminal: **ECHO on, ICANON on** — sudo relays nothing and the command's own settings show through. With one: **ECHO OFF, ICANON OFF**. The same command with no `sudo` in front reads ECHO on/ICANON on either way, so it is sudo and not the ctty. `su` does **not** do this ([#339](#) still stands). The rule this produces: **termios is trustworthy exactly where `/proc` is** — a job the node can read is a job with nothing relaying for it. Where the privilege wall hides the process it also inserts the relay, so an inference made there must ignore termios and go on the text alone. Reading it anyway called `sudo pacman`'s `[Y/n]` a full-screen program and dropped it off the board |
| 342 | **A pane's very first foreground job is not its shell.** For the first few hundred milliseconds of a fresh herdr pane, `pane.process_info` names a transient helper the operator's `bashrc` spawns beside the shell — so the first name a node reports for a pane is a real name for a job that is about to be gone | A throwaway `tailprobe` session, one `workspace.create`, and `pane.process_info` sampled at t+0.2/0.6/1.2/2.5/4.0 s | At **t+0.2 s**: `foreground_processes: ['bash', 'gawk']`. At every later sample: `['bash']`. The helper varies — `gawk` in this run, **`tail`** in the two suite runs that surfaced this — because it is whatever ble.sh happens to be running as it initialises ([#297](#) is the same shell, seen from the other end). Nothing is wrong with the name: `kampr · tail` is the correct name for that instant. What is wrong is reading the *first* one and calling it settled, which is what made `a_node_told_to_report_names_puts_one_on_the_pane_at_the_desk` fail about twice in twelve suite runs |
| 343 | **`claude agents --json` lists the interactive sessions on this machine — including ones the caller did not start — with pid, session id and a live status, needs no TTY and needs no configuration** | `claude agents --json` and `claude agents --help` on 2.1.251, against three sessions started by hand | A JSON array of `{pid, cwd, kind, startedAt, sessionId, name, status}`; the three live sessions read `busy`, `idle`, `idle`, matching what they were doing. `--help` calls it *"Print active sessions (interactive and background) as a JSON array and exit (for scripting; does not require a TTY)"* — an explicit third-party contract the transcript JSONL does not have, and the only **documented** Claude surface of the three this log now records. **Not observed**: the `state` and `waitingFor` fields reported elsewhere as documented did not appear for interactive sessions on this build |
| 344 | **`~/.claude/sessions/<pid>.json`'s `status` tracks a live session, is written in place, and carries four values plus a reason** — closing the question [#311](#) left open | compared `statusUpdatedAt` against `startedAt` on three live sessions; watched a fourth from creation at 0.4 s sampling; drove a throwaway session through a turn and a `/model` dialog at 100 ms sampling | Drift past `startedAt` of **9352 min, 86.5 min and 132.9 min** — it is not stamped once. A new session's marker appears with **no `status` field** and is rewritten `idle` 0.4 s later. `status` ∈ `busy \| shell \| idle \| waiting`, with `waitingFor` ∈ `"input needed" \| "worker request" \| "sandbox request" \| "dialog open" \| <the top dialog's label>`; `shell` is idle with a background shell task. Transitions land in **~100 ms**. The write is `publishDiscipline: "inPlace"` — **inode unchanged across every flip** — so `IN_MODIFY` on the file is a push feed needing no handshake and no socket of one's own. This is the state herdr's scrape structurally cannot reach: a pane blocked on a prompt looks exactly like one that has finished |
| 345 | **Claude never holds its transcript open, so no `lsof`-shaped handle finds it** | sampled `/proc/<pid>/fd` 60 times on a session that was actively writing its transcript | The `.jsonl` appeared **0 of 60 times** — it opens, appends and closes. The fd table does hold an open directory handle `/tmp/claude-<uid>/<slug>/<sessionId>/tasks`, which is kernel-held and cannot go stale, but the marker is already `procStart`-validated so it closes no hole the marker leaves, and it is an unversioned scratch layout. The marker is the handle; this is why [`crate::codex`]'s fd is not a general answer |
| 346 | **A live Claude session listens on `/run/user/<uid>/cc-socks/<pid>.sock`, and `notify_idle` is a real push — but it cannot say "blocked", which is the one state worth pushing** | read `messagingSocketPath`/`peerProtocol`/`peerFeatures` off live markers; then, against a throwaway session, connected with no auth line, registered `notify_when_idle`, and drove the session to a `/model` dialog and back | One `srw-------` socket per live pid; `peerProtocol: 1` advertised and **never read by any parser**; `peerFeatures: ["notify_idle", "reply_across_default_dirs", "artifact_yield"]`. Newline-delimited JSON, **one direction** — a passive read gets EOF at exactly 30 s and nothing else. On Linux **auth is optional**; the `0700` directory and `SO_PEERCRED` are the access control. `notify_when_idle` is a one-shot subscription answered by the session **dialling out** to the subscriber's own socket, and the reply only arrives if the process that listens is the process that subscribed. **It is wired off two booleans — `idle\|shell` and `busy` — so a session sitting at a dialog fires nothing at all**: 40 s of silence while blocked, then a notice 750 ms after the dialog closed. `state: "exited"` does fire, within 10 ms of `SIGTERM`. The target also prints the subscription into its own transcript, so it is not a silent watch |
| 347 | **The whole cross-session socket surface is behind a server-side kill switch that fails quiet** | static read of the gate in the 2.1.251 bundle | `CLAUDE_CODE_HARBOR_KITE` env override falling through to a remotely-flippable `tengu_harbor_kite` flag, default true, with `tengu_harbor_kite_mode_emit` and a tunable `tengu_harbor_kite_limits` beside it. When it flips, subscriptions return `refuse` and **go quiet rather than erroring** — the [#233](#) shape, on a machine that worked yesterday. The marker file is the conservative half of the pair and is what a node should depend on |
| 348 | **Any same-uid process can inject a real user turn into any live Claude Code session with no authentication** | wrote `{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":"…"}}` to a throwaway session's `cc-socks` socket with no auth frame | The turn ran and was answered. The inbound gate defaults to **accept** when unset and only escalates when the session is in `bypassPermissions`; the socket directory being `0700` is the entire access control. The session renders a peer banner, so it is not silent — but it is not gated either. A fact about any machine a node runs on, and relevant to the threat model rather than to any one surface |
| 349 | **Codex DOES publish a process-to-thread map, and it is the same kernel-backed handle `agy` uses: `~/.codex/thread-writer-locks/<thread-id>.lock` is held under `flock` for the whole life of the session** — this crate's adapter said it published none | throwaway `CODEX_HOME`, `codex` under a pty for 62 s, `/proc/<pid>/fd` and `/proc/locks` sampled every 60 ms — 865 samples | One `FLOCK ADVISORY WRITE` whose inode `stat` proves is the thread's lock file, present from **t=1.805 — before any prompt** — to process death, **840 samples, zero gaps**. The lock files are **empty**, which is what made them look like nothing worth reading. The holder is the **native** `codex` binary, not the `bin/codex.js` wrapper that spawns it, so a pipeline walk has to reach the child. The lock *files* are never unlinked, so the directory is a list of threads that have **ended** and only the lock table separates them — identical in shape to `agy`'s `presence/` |
| 350 | **`/new` makes a codex process hold TWO thread locks at once — it does not move the lock the way `agy` does** | the same setup, two `/new`s and no prompts | `t=1.82` one lock; `t=31.2` **two**, both held by the one pid, the first never released. So `presence::holder`'s "exactly one held lock or answer nothing" rule would refuse every codex session that has ever used `/new`, and the **newest lock file** is what disambiguates — which is what `presence::newest_holder` exists for |
| 351 | **Codex also holds its rollout open, so `/proc/<pid>/fd` names its transcript directly** — the opposite of Claude ([#345](#)) | the same 865 samples | The rollout appears in the fd table at first prompt and stays for **696 of 696 samples after, zero gaps**. Codex 0.150.1 has moved its state into SQLite (`state_5.sqlite`'s `threads.rollout_path`, `thread_history_1.sqlite`'s `thread_turns.status`, which reads `inProgress` for a whole turn including a 38 s approval hold and then `completed`) while **still** writing the rollout JSONL. The status column does **not** separate blocked from working; the unmatched tool call of [#43](#) is still the only pending signal |
| 352 | **`agy`'s presence lock does not exist until the first prompt, so an agy pane with an empty conversation has no handle at all** | two `agy` runs under a pty in a throwaway `HOME`, `/proc/locks` and the state dir sampled every 60–100 ms | `presence/<conv-id>.lock` appears at **t=18.29** and **t=18.24**, at the first submit, never at boot — absent for the ~14 s the process was up and idle. Claude's marker is written at session *open*; this is not. `agy` does write a pid-keyed file at boot — `crashes/crash_<pid>_<uuid>.log`, fd held for 782 of 783 samples and **unlinked on clean exit** — which names no conversation but covers exactly that window |
| 353 | **`agy` publishes the strongest status signal of the four harnesses: `conversations/<id>.db`'s `steps.status` separates generating from awaiting-approval as a value rather than an inference** | a turn driven to a shell-permission prompt and held 57 s, `steps` polled every 100 ms | `step_type 15` sat at **status 8** while the model generated and became **3** when it finished; `step_type 132` sat at **status 9** for the whole 57 s the permission prompt was on screen and became **6** when interrupted. Settled transcripts on disk carry only 3 and 6, which is why the running values are invisible to a post-hoc read. **The enum is only partly mapped** — 3, 6, 8 and 9 are all that were observed, which is why nothing reads it yet |
